- [ ] **Step 3: Write `ops/flow.rs`**

```rust
//! Jumps, calls, returns, and the conditions that gate them.

use crate::flags::{C, PV, S, Z};
use crate::ops::load;
use crate::{Bus, Z80};

/// Evaluates condition code `i`: `NZ Z NC C PO PE P M`.
///
/// Each pair is a flag and its negation, in that order — so the low bit selects
/// "flag set" and the upper two select which flag.
#[must_use]
pub fn cond(cpu: &Z80, i: u8) -> bool {
    let flag = match i >> 1 {
        0 => Z,
        1 => C,
        2 => PV,
        3 => S,
        _ => unreachable!("condition {i} is not three bits"),
    };
    (cpu.f & flag != 0) == (i & 1 != 0)
}

/// Applies a signed displacement to `PC`, which already points past the operand.
pub fn jr(cpu: &mut Z80, d: u8) {
    cpu.pc = cpu.pc.wrapping_add(i16::from(d as i8) as u16);
}

/// Pushes the return address and jumps.
pub fn call<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, target: u16) {
    load::push(cpu, bus, cpu.pc);
    cpu.pc = target;
}

/// Pops the return address.
pub fn ret<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) {
    cpu.pc = load::pop(cpu, bus);
}

/// `RST n`: a one-byte call to `n * 8`.
pub fn rst<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, n: u8) {
    call(cpu, bus, u16::from(n) * 8);
}
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the accumulator rotates in `ops/bits.rs`**

```rust
//! Rotates, shifts, and bit operations.
//!
//! The four accumulator rotates here are **not** the `CB` page's versions of the
//! same operations. `RLCA` writes carry, H, N, F3 and F5, preserving S, Z and
//! P/V; `RLC A` on the `CB` page writes all eight. Both are one byte, both exist,
//! and the flag difference is the entire reason.

use crate::flags::{C, F3, F5, S, Z, PV};
use crate::Z80;

/// The flag pattern the four accumulator rotates share: carry from the rotated-out
/// bit, F3/F5 from the result, H and N cleared, S/Z/P-V preserved.
fn rot_a_flags(cpu: &mut Z80, result: u8, carry: bool) {
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & (S | Z | PV)) | (result & (F5 | F3)) | u8::from(carry);
    cpu.q = 1;
}

/// `RLCA`: bit 7 to bit 0 and to carry.
pub fn rlca(cpu: &mut Z80) {
    let carry = cpu.a & 0x80 != 0;
    cpu.a = cpu.a.rotate_left(1);
    rot_a_flags(cpu, cpu.a, carry);
}

/// `RRCA`: bit 0 to bit 7 and to carry.
pub fn rrca(cpu: &mut Z80) {
    let carry = cpu.a & 0x01 != 0;
    cpu.a = cpu.a.rotate_right(1);
    rot_a_flags(cpu, cpu.a, carry);
}

/// `RLA`: a nine-bit rotate through carry.
pub fn rla(cpu: &mut Z80) {
    let carry_in = cpu.f & C;
    let carry_out = cpu.a & 0x80 != 0;
    cpu.a = (cpu.a << 1) | carry_in;
    rot_a_flags(cpu, cpu.a, carry_out);
}

/// `RRA`: [`rla`] mirrored.
pub fn rra(cpu: &mut Z80) {
    let carry_in = (cpu.f & C) << 7;
    let carry_out = cpu.a & 0x01 != 0;
    cpu.a = (cpu.a >> 1) | carry_in;
    rot_a_flags(cpu, cpu.a, carry_out);
}
```

- [ ] **Step 5: Add the decode arms**

```rust
        // The four accumulator rotates.
        0x07 => { bits::rlca(cpu); 4 }
        0x0F => { bits::rrca(cpu); 4 }
        0x17 => { bits::rla(cpu); 4 }
        0x1F => { bits::rra(cpu); 4 }
        // JR d, and the four conditional forms. The condition index for
        // 0x20/0x28/0x30/0x38 is bits 4-3 of the opcode, which encode NZ Z NC C.
        0x18 => {
            let d = cpu.imm(bus);
            flow::jr(cpu, d);
            cpu.q = 0;
            12
        }
        0x20 | 0x28 | 0x30 | 0x38 => {
            let d = cpu.imm(bus);
            cpu.q = 0;
            if flow::cond(cpu, (op >> 3) & 3) {
                flow::jr(cpu, d);
                12
            } else {
                7
            }
        }
        0x10 => {
            // DJNZ. B is decremented without flags -- this is not `dec8`, and
            // using it here would clobber a comparison the loop body depends on.
            let d = cpu.imm(bus);
            cpu.b = cpu.b.wrapping_sub(1);
            cpu.q = 0;
            if cpu.b != 0 {
                flow::jr(cpu, d);
                13
            } else {
                8
            }
        }
        0xC3 => {
            cpu.pc = cpu.imm16(bus);
            cpu.q = 0;
            10
        }
        // JP cc,nn: 10 T-states either way -- the operand is read regardless, and
        // there is nothing else to charge for.
        0xC2 | 0xCA | 0xD2 | 0xDA | 0xE2 | 0xEA | 0xF2 | 0xFA => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            if flow::cond(cpu, (op >> 3) & 7) {
                cpu.pc = nn;
            }
            cpu.q = 0;
            10
        }
        0xE9 => {
            // JP (HL): the target is HL itself. The parentheses are Zilog's, and
            // they are wrong.
            cpu.pc = cpu.hl();
            cpu.q = 0;
            4
        }
        0xCD => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            flow::call(cpu, bus, nn);
            cpu.q = 0;
            17
        }
        0xC4 | 0xCC | 0xD4 | 0xDC | 0xE4 | 0xEC | 0xF4 | 0xFC => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            cpu.q = 0;
            if flow::cond(cpu, (op >> 3) & 7) {
                flow::call(cpu, bus, nn);
                17
            } else {
                10
            }
        }
        0xC9 => {
            flow::ret(cpu, bus);
            cpu.q = 0;
            10
        }
        0xC0 | 0xC8 | 0xD0 | 0xD8 | 0xE0 | 0xE8 | 0xF0 | 0xF8 => {
            cpu.q = 0;
            if flow::cond(cpu, (op >> 3) & 7) {
                flow::ret(cpu, bus);
                11
            } else {
                5
            }
        }
        0xC7 | 0xCF | 0xD7 | 0xDF | 0xE7 | 0xEF | 0xF7 | 0xFF => {
            flow::rst(cpu, bus, (op >> 3) & 7);
            cpu.q = 0;
            11
        }
        // EX (SP),HL: the word at the top of the stack, swapped with HL.
        0xE3 => {
            let lo = bus.read(cpu.sp);
            let hi = bus.read(cpu.sp.wrapping_add(1));
            bus.write(cpu.sp, cpu.l);
            bus.write(cpu.sp.wrapping_add(1), cpu.h);
            cpu.h = hi;
            cpu.l = lo;
            cpu.q = 0;
            19
        }
        // IN A,(n) and OUT (n),A. The port's high byte is A, not zero -- the
        // reason `Bus::port_in` takes 16 bits.
        0xDB => {
            let n = cpu.imm(bus);
            let port = u16::from(cpu.a) << 8 | u16::from(n);
            cpu.a = bus.port_in(port);
            cpu.q = 0;
            11
        }
        0xD3 => {
            let n = cpu.imm(bus);
            let port = u16::from(cpu.a) << 8 | u16::from(n);
            bus.port_out(port, cpu.a);
            cpu.q = 0;
            11
        }
```

```bash
Conditional instructions have two T-state costs and both are vector
files: CALL is 17 taken and 10 not, RET 11 and 5, JR 12 and 7, DJNZ 13
and 8. A core returning one number for both passes every register
assertion and fails half its cases with a correct result.

DJNZ decrements B without writing flags -- not dec8 -- so a loop can
carry a comparison across its body. RLCA is not RLC A: it preserves S, Z
and P/V while the CB page's version writes them, which is why both
one-byte forms exist.

JP (HL) does not dereference HL. Zilog's parentheses are wrong and a core
that believed them would jump somewhere plausible.
```

---

## Task 9: The `CB` page

**Files:**
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/ops/bits.rs` (the shifts and `BIT`/`SET`/`RES`)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/decode.rs` (add `cb_page`)

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `decode::{reg, set_reg}`, `flags::*`.
- Produces: `ops::bits::{rot, RotOp, bit_test}` and `decode::cb_page`.
  `RotOp` is the eight-way encoding `RLC RRC RL RR SLA SRA SLL SRL`.

### The page's shape, and its one undocumented member

256 opcodes, four quarters: `00..=3F` the eight rotate/shift operations over the
eight register slots, `40..=7F` `BIT b,r`, `80..=BF` `RES b,r`, `C0..=FF`
`SET b,r`. Bits 5–3 are the bit number; bits 2–0 the register.

`SLL` (`30..=37`) is **undocumented** — a shift left that puts a 1 in bit 0 rather
than a 0. It is in the suite, it has a file, and there is nothing to decide about
it.

### `BIT b,(HL)`'s flags are the reason this task has hand-written tests

`BIT` takes F3 and F5 from the **operand** for register forms — but for `(HL)`,
F3/F5 come from the internal `W` latch's high byte, which for `BIT b,(HL)` is the
high byte of `HL`. That is the kind of detail that is only visible in `wz`, which
the suite compares. The hand-written test below pins the register form; the `(HL)`
form is verified by the `cb 46` vector file, at Task 15.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// The eight rotate and shift operations, each at the boundary that
    /// distinguishes it from its neighbours.
    ///
    /// `SLA` and `SLL` differ in exactly one bit of the result, and `SRA` and `SRL`
    /// in exactly one — so each pair is tested on a value where that bit shows.
    #[test]
    fn the_eight_rotates_and_shifts_differ_where_they_should() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();

        // RLC: bit 7 wraps to bit 0 and to carry.
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Rlc, 0x85), 0x0B);
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C);

        // RL: bit 7 to carry, the old carry to bit 0. Nine bits.
        c.f = C;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Rl, 0x85), 0x0B, "0x85<<1 | old carry");
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Rl, 0x85), 0x0A, "without the carry in");

        // RRC and RR, mirrored.
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Rrc, 0x01), 0x80);
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C);
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Rr, 0x01), 0x00, "no carry in, so bit 7 is 0");
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C, "and bit 0 went to carry");

        // SLA puts 0 in bit 0; SLL -- undocumented -- puts 1.
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Sla, 0x80), 0x00);
        assert_eq!(c.f & (C | Z), C | Z);
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Sll, 0x80), 0x01, "SLL's bit 0 is one");
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, 0, "so the result is not zero");

        // SRA preserves the sign; SRL clears bit 7.
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Sra, 0x85), 0xC2, "bit 7 is copied down");
        assert_eq!(c.f & (S | C), S | C);
        c.f = 0;
        assert_eq!(rot(&mut c, RotOp::Srl, 0x85), 0x42, "bit 7 becomes zero");
        assert_eq!(c.f & S, 0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C);
    }

    /// All eight write S, Z, P/V, F3 and F5 from the result, and clear H and N.
    ///
    /// The contrast with the accumulator rotates, asserted rather than described:
    /// those preserve S, Z and P/V, and these do not.
    #[test]
    fn every_cb_rotate_writes_the_full_flag_set() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        for op in [
            RotOp::Rlc, RotOp::Rrc, RotOp::Rl, RotOp::Rr,
            RotOp::Sla, RotOp::Sra, RotOp::Sll, RotOp::Srl,
        ] {
            c.f = S | Z | PV | H | N;
            let r = rot(&mut c, op, 0x02);
            assert_eq!(c.f & (H | N), 0, "{op:?} must clear H and N");
            assert_eq!(
                c.f & (S | Z | PV | F5 | F3),
                crate::flags::sz53p(r),
                "{op:?} must write S/Z/P-V/F5/F3 from the result {r:#04X}"
            );
        }
    }

    /// `BIT b,r` sets Z when the bit is clear, and takes F3/F5 from the operand.
    ///
    /// H is always set and N always cleared — `BIT` is specified as an `AND` with a
    /// mask, and `AND` sets H. P/V is a copy of Z, which is the detail cores miss.
    #[test]
    fn bit_tests_a_bit_and_copies_z_into_parity() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.f = 0;
        bit_test(&mut c, 3, 0x08);
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, 0, "bit 3 of 0x08 is set, so Z is clear");
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, 0, "and P/V follows Z");
        assert_eq!(c.f & H, H, "H is always set");
        assert_eq!(c.f & N, 0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & F3, F3, "F3/F5 come from the operand 0x08");

        c.f = 0;
        bit_test(&mut c, 4, 0x08);
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, Z, "bit 4 is clear");
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "and P/V is a copy of Z, not a parity");

        // Bit 7 set puts S in the flags -- the one bit position where BIT differs
        // from the others, because S is a copy of result bit 7 and the result of
        // `BIT 7,r` has bit 7 set exactly when the tested bit was.
        c.f = 0;
        bit_test(&mut c, 7, 0x80);
        assert_eq!(c.f & S, S, "BIT 7 on a negative value sets S");
        c.f = 0;
        bit_test(&mut c, 6, 0xC0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & S, 0, "and BIT 6 does not, even on the same value");
    }

    /// `SET` and `RES` touch no flags whatsoever.
    #[test]
    fn set_and_res_write_no_flags() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.b = 0x00;
        c.f = 0x5A;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xCB, 0xC0]); // SET 0,B
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8);
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0x01);
        assert_eq!(c.f, 0x5A, "no flags");

        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xCB, 0x80]); // RES 0,B
        c.pc = 0x100;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8);
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0x00);
        assert_eq!(c.f, 0x5A);
    }

    /// The page's T-state costs: 8 for a register, 15 for `(HL)`, and **12 for
    /// `BIT b,(HL)`** — which writes nothing back and so is three cheaper.
    #[test]
    fn the_cb_page_costs_eight_fifteen_or_twelve() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xCB, 0x00]); // RLC B
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8, "a register form");

        c.pc = 0x100;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xCB, 0x06]); // RLC (HL)
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 15, "read, modify, write");

        c.pc = 0x100;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xCB, 0x46]); // BIT 0,(HL)
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 12, "BIT writes nothing back");
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Extend `ops/bits.rs`**

```rust
/// The `CB` page's eight rotate and shift operations, in their encoded order.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, Debug)]
pub enum RotOp {
    Rlc,
    Rrc,
    Rl,
    Rr,
    Sla,
    Sra,
    /// Undocumented: a left shift that puts **1** in bit 0.
    ///
    /// Absent from the Zilog manual, present in every real Z80, and shipped as
    /// eight vector files. There is nothing to decide about it.
    Sll,
    Srl,
}

impl RotOp {
    /// Decodes bits 5–3 of a `CB`-page opcode.
    #[must_use]
    pub fn from_index(i: u8) -> Self {
        match i {
            0 => RotOp::Rlc,
            1 => RotOp::Rrc,
            2 => RotOp::Rl,
            3 => RotOp::Rr,
            4 => RotOp::Sla,
            5 => RotOp::Sra,
            6 => RotOp::Sll,
            7 => RotOp::Srl,
            _ => unreachable!("rotate index {i} is not three bits"),
        }
    }
}

/// Applies a `CB`-page rotate or shift, writing the full flag set.
///
/// All eight write S, Z, P/V (as parity), F3 and F5 from the result and clear H
/// and N — unlike the accumulator rotates, which preserve S, Z and P/V.
#[must_use]
pub fn rot(cpu: &mut Z80, which: RotOp, v: u8) -> u8 {
    let old_c = cpu.f & C;
    let (r, carry) = match which {
        RotOp::Rlc => (v.rotate_left(1), v & 0x80 != 0),
        RotOp::Rrc => (v.rotate_right(1), v & 0x01 != 0),
        RotOp::Rl => ((v << 1) | old_c, v & 0x80 != 0),
        RotOp::Rr => ((v >> 1) | (old_c << 7), v & 0x01 != 0),
        RotOp::Sla => (v << 1, v & 0x80 != 0),
        RotOp::Sra => ((v >> 1) | (v & 0x80), v & 0x01 != 0),
        RotOp::Sll => ((v << 1) | 1, v & 0x80 != 0),
        RotOp::Srl => (v >> 1, v & 0x01 != 0),
    };
    cpu.f = crate::flags::sz53p(r) | u8::from(carry);
    cpu.q = 1;
    r
}

/// `BIT b,v`: sets Z when bit `b` of `v` is clear.
///
/// H is always set (the operation is an `AND` with a mask, and `AND` sets H), N is
/// cleared, **P/V is a copy of Z** rather than a parity, and S is set only by
/// `BIT 7` on a value whose bit 7 is set — because S is a copy of the result's bit
/// 7 and the result is `v & (1 << b)`.
pub fn bit_test(cpu: &mut Z80, b: u8, v: u8) {
    let masked = v & (1 << b);
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & C)
        | H
        | (v & (F5 | F3))
        | if masked == 0 { Z | PV } else { 0 }
        | (masked & S);
    cpu.q = 1;
}
```

The `use` line at the top of `bits.rs` grows to
`use crate::flags::{C, F3, F5, H, N, PV, S, Z};`.

- [ ] **Step 4: Add `cb_page` to `decode.rs`**

```rust
/// The `CB` page: rotates and shifts, then `BIT`, `RES` and `SET`.
///
/// `BIT b,(HL)` is 12 T-states rather than 15 because it writes nothing back —
/// the one asymmetry on an otherwise uniform page.
pub fn cb_page<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) -> u32 {
    let op = cpu.fetch(bus);
    let (slot, bit) = (op & 7, (op >> 3) & 7);
    let mem = slot == 6;
    match op {
        0x00..=0x3F => {
            let v = reg(cpu, bus, slot);
            let r = bits::rot(cpu, bits::RotOp::from_index(bit), v);
            set_reg(cpu, bus, slot, r);
            if mem { 15 } else { 8 }
        }
        0x40..=0x7F => {
            let v = reg(cpu, bus, slot);
            bits::bit_test(cpu, bit, v);
            if mem { 12 } else { 8 }
        }
        0x80..=0xBF => {
            let v = reg(cpu, bus, slot);
            set_reg(cpu, bus, slot, v & !(1 << bit));
            cpu.q = 0;
            if mem { 15 } else { 8 }
        }
        0xC0..=0xFF => {
            let v = reg(cpu, bus, slot);
            set_reg(cpu, bus, slot, v | (1 << bit));
            cpu.q = 0;
            if mem { 15 } else { 8 }
        }
    }
}
```

and the base-page arm that reaches it:

```rust
        0xCB => cb_page(cpu, bus),
```

```bash
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "feat(z80): the CB page, SLL included

256 opcodes in four uniform quarters, with one asymmetry: BIT b,(HL) is
12 T-states, not 15, because it writes nothing back.

BIT's P/V is a copy of Z rather than a parity, and its S is set only by
BIT 7 on a negative value -- both fall out of the operation being an AND
with a mask, and both are what cores get wrong.

SLL is undocumented, shifts a 1 into bit 0, and has eight vector files.
There was nothing to decide about it.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 10: The `ED` page — block moves, block I/O, and 16-bit `ADC`/`SBC`

**Files:**
- Create: `crates/z80/src/ops/io.rs`
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/ops/load.rs` (the block transfers)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/ops/alu.rs` (`adc16`, `sbc16`, `neg`)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/ops/bits.rs` (`RLD`, `RRD`)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/decode.rs` (add `ed_page`)

**Interfaces:**
- Produces: `ops::load::{ldi, ldd, cpi, cpd}`, `ops::io::{ini, ind, outi, outd, in_r_c, out_c_r}`,
  `ops::alu::{adc16, sbc16, neg}`, `ops::bits::{rld, rrd}`, `decode::ed_page`.

### The 80 files, and why the repeating forms are not loops

`LDIR` is `LDI` that re-executes itself: when `BC` is still non-zero it decrements
`PC` by two so the same instruction is fetched again, costing 21 T-states instead
of 16. **It is not a loop inside `step`.** Writing it as one would make a
`LDIR` of 65,536 bytes a single un-interruptible `step`, which is wrong on real
hardware (an interrupt can land between iterations) and impossible to verify
against single-instruction vectors.

### The flag rules that are only on this page

- `LDI`/`LDD` put **`BC != 0`** in P/V and take F3/F5 from a *computed* byte:
  `A + the transferred value`, bit 1 into F5 and bit 3 into F3. Not from the
  result, not from the operand — from a sum that exists nowhere else.
- `CPI`/`CPD` take F3/F5 from `A - value - H`, by the same trick.
- `IN r,(C)` sets flags; `IN A,(n)` — on the base page — does not. Same
  instruction name, different flag behaviour.
- `NEG` is `0 - A` with subtraction flags, and its P/V is set only for `A = 0x80`.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// `LDI` moves a byte, advances both pointers, decrements `BC`.
    ///
    /// And its P/V is `BC != 0` **after** the decrement — a repeat-count flag, not
    /// a parity. Every other instruction on the chip that writes P/V writes either
    /// parity or overflow; this one writes neither.
    #[test]
    fn ldi_moves_a_byte_and_puts_the_remaining_count_in_parity() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        c.set_de(0x3000);
        c.set_bc(2);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0xA0]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x5A;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 16);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x3000], 0x5A);
        assert_eq!(c.hl(), 0x2001);
        assert_eq!(c.de(), 0x3001);
        assert_eq!(c.bc(), 1);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "BC is 1, so P/V is set");
        assert_eq!(c.f & N, 0, "N and H are cleared");
        assert_eq!(c.f & H, 0);

        // One more, and BC reaches zero.
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.bc(), 0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, 0, "BC is 0, so P/V is clear");
    }

    /// `LDI`'s F3/F5 come from `A + the byte moved`, bit 3 and bit 1.
    ///
    /// Bit **1** into F5, not bit 5. The undocumented bits are not copies here —
    /// they are two bits of a sum that appears in no register — and this is the
    /// detail the `ed a0` vector file exists to catch.
    #[test]
    fn ldi_derives_the_undocumented_flags_from_a_plus_the_moved_byte() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        c.set_de(0x3000);
        c.set_bc(1);
        c.a = 0x00;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0xA0]);
        // 0x00 + 0x0A = 0x0A: bit 3 set, bit 1 set.
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x0A;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.f & F3, F3, "bit 3 of the sum goes to F3");
        assert_eq!(c.f & F5, F5, "and bit 1 of the sum goes to F5");

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        c.set_de(0x3000);
        c.set_bc(1);
        c.a = 0x00;
        // 0x00 + 0x04 = 0x04: bit 3 clear, bit 1 clear.
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x04;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.f & (F3 | F5), 0, "and both clear when the sum's bits are");
    }

    /// `LDIR` re-executes by rewinding `PC`, and costs 21 while it repeats.
    ///
    /// Not a loop: each iteration is one `step`, so an interrupt can land between
    /// them — as it can on the real chip — and each is one vector case.
    #[test]
    fn ldir_rewinds_pc_to_repeat_rather_than_looping_internally() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        c.set_de(0x3000);
        c.set_bc(2);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0xB0]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x11;
        m.ram[0x2001] = 0x22;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 21, "repeating costs 21");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x100, "and PC is back on the ED, ready to re-fetch");
        assert_eq!(c.bc(), 1);
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 16, "the last iteration costs 16");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x102, "and PC finally moves past the instruction");
        assert_eq!(c.bc(), 0);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x3000], 0x11);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x3001], 0x22);
    }

    /// `CPI` compares without writing `A`, and its carry is untouched.
    #[test]
    fn cpi_compares_and_leaves_carry_alone() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.a = 0x5A;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        c.set_bc(1);
        c.f = C;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0xA1]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x5A;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 16);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x5A, "A is not written");
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, Z, "the bytes matched");
        assert_eq!(c.f & N, N, "CPI is a subtraction");
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C, "and carry is preserved, unlike CP");
        assert_eq!(c.hl(), 0x2001);
        assert_eq!(c.bc(), 0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, 0, "BC reached zero");
    }

    /// 16-bit `ADC HL,rr` writes the **full** flag set, unlike `ADD HL,rr`.
    ///
    /// S, Z and P/V are all written here and all preserved by `ADD HL,rr`. Two
    /// instructions, one operation, different flags — and `ADD` is on the base page
    /// while `ADC` is on `ED`, which is the only clue in the encoding.
    #[test]
    fn adc16_writes_sign_zero_and_overflow_where_add16_preserves_them() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.f = 0;
        let r = adc16(&mut c, 0x0000, 0x0000);
        assert_eq!(r, 0);
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, Z, "ADC HL,rr sets Z -- ADD HL,rr never does");
        assert_eq!(c.f & N, 0);

        c.f = 0;
        let r = adc16(&mut c, 0x7FFF, 0x0001);
        assert_eq!(r, 0x8000);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "and it reports signed overflow");
        assert_eq!(c.f & S, S);

        c.f = C;
        let r = adc16(&mut c, 0x0000, 0x0000);
        assert_eq!(r, 1, "and it adds the carry in");
    }

    /// `SBC HL,rr` sets N and borrows.
    #[test]
    fn sbc16_sets_n_and_reports_a_borrow() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.f = 0;
        let r = sbc16(&mut c, 0x0000, 0x0001);
        assert_eq!(r, 0xFFFF);
        assert_eq!(c.f & (N | C | S), N | C | S);

        c.f = 0;
        let r = sbc16(&mut c, 0x8000, 0x0001);
        assert_eq!(r, 0x7FFF);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "-32768 - 1 overflows");
    }

    /// `NEG` is `0 - A`, and overflows only at 0x80.
    ///
    /// 0x80 is the one value with no positive counterpart in eight bits, so it is
    /// the only input that sets P/V — a single-value condition, which is exactly
    /// the kind a core implements as `false` and never notices.
    #[test]
    fn neg_overflows_only_at_the_most_negative_value() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.a = 0x80;
        c.f = 0;
        neg(&mut c);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x80, "negating -128 gives -128");
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "which is the overflow");
        assert_eq!(c.f & (N | C | S), N | C | S);

        c.a = 0x01;
        c.f = 0;
        neg(&mut c);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0xFF);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, 0, "and no other value does");
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C, "a non-zero A borrows");

        c.a = 0x00;
        c.f = 0;
        neg(&mut c);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x00);
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, Z);
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, 0, "zero is the only A that does not borrow");
    }

    /// `IN r,(C)` sets flags; `IN A,(n)` does not.
    ///
    /// Same mnemonic family, different pages, different behaviour. A core that
    /// shared one implementation would fail one of the two and look correct on the
    /// other.
    #[test]
    fn in_from_c_sets_flags_but_in_a_from_n_does_not() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.b = 0x12;
        c.c = 0x34;
        c.f = 0xFF;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0x40]); // IN B,(C)
        m.port_in_value = 0x00;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 12);
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0x00);
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, Z, "IN r,(C) flags the byte it read");
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "with parity");
        assert_eq!(c.f & (H | N), 0);

        // IN A,(n) on the base page: flags untouched.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.a = 0x12;
        c.f = 0x5A;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDB, 0x34]);
        m.port_in_value = 0x00;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x00);
        assert_eq!(c.f, 0x5A, "and this form writes no flags at all");
    }

    /// `IN r,(C)` and `OUT (C),r` use `BC` as the port, `B` on the high half.
    #[test]
    fn the_c_port_forms_use_bc_as_a_sixteen_bit_address() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.b = 0x12;
        c.c = 0x34;
        c.d = 0x99;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0x51]); // OUT (C),D
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 12);
        assert_eq!(m.ports_out, vec![(0x1234, 0x99)], "BC, not just C");
    }

    /// `RLD` rotates a nibble through `A` and `(HL)`.
    ///
    /// Four bits of `A` and eight of `(HL)` in a twelve-bit rotation, for BCD
    /// arithmetic. Hand-computed: A=0x7A, (HL)=0x31 gives A=0x73, (HL)=0x1A.
    #[test]
    fn rld_rotates_twelve_bits_through_a_and_memory() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.a = 0x7A;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0x6F]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x31;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 18);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x73, "A's low nibble took (HL)'s high nibble");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2000], 0x1A, "and (HL) shifted up, taking A's old low");
        assert_eq!(c.f & (H | N), 0);
    }

    /// `RRD` is `RLD`'s mirror: A=0x84, (HL)=0x20 gives A=0x80, (HL)=0x42.
    #[test]
    fn rrd_rotates_the_other_way() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.a = 0x84;
        c.set_hl(0x2000);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0x67]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x20;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 18);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x80);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2000], 0x42);
    }

    /// `LD A,I` sets `P`, and copies IFF2 into P/V.
    ///
    /// The interrupt-state leak: P/V holds IFF2 so software can read the interrupt
    /// enable, which is otherwise invisible. `p` records that this instruction ran,
    /// because an interrupt arriving here corrupts the flag on real hardware.
    #[test]
    fn ld_a_i_copies_iff2_into_parity_and_sets_p() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.i = 0x42;
        c.iff2 = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, 0x57]);
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 9);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0x42);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "IFF2 was set");
        assert_eq!(c.p, 1, "and P marks this instruction as the last one run");

        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.iff2 = false;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, 0);
    }

    /// `IM 0`, `IM 1` and `IM 2` set the mode and nothing else.
    #[test]
    fn the_im_instructions_set_the_interrupt_mode() {
        for (op, mode) in [(0x46u8, 0u8), (0x56, 1), (0x5E, 2)] {
            let mut c = Z80::new();
            c.pc = 0x100;
            c.im = 3; // an impossible value, so a no-op would show
            let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xED, op]);
            assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8);
            assert_eq!(c.im, mode, "ED {op:#04X}");
        }
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `ops/io.rs`**

```rust
//! The I/O-space instructions.
//!
//! Two families with different flag behaviour: `IN A,(n)` and `OUT (n),A` on the
//! base page write no flags, while `IN r,(C)` and the block forms on the `ED` page
//! do. Sharing an implementation between them is the mistake this split prevents.

use crate::flags::{self, C, H, N, PV, S, Z};
use crate::{Bus, Z80};

/// `IN r,(C)`: reads port `BC` and **flags the byte**.
#[must_use]
pub fn in_r_c<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) -> u8 {
    let v = bus.port_in(cpu.bc());
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & C) | flags::sz53p(v);
    cpu.q = 1;
    v
}

/// `OUT (C),r`: writes `v` to port `BC`. No flags.
pub fn out_c_r<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, v: u8) {
    bus.port_out(cpu.bc(), v);
    cpu.q = 0;
}

/// `INI` / `IND`: read a port into `(HL)`, step `HL`, decrement `B`.
///
/// `inc` selects the direction. The flags are the block-I/O family's own peculiar
/// set: Z from `B`, N from bit 7 of the byte read, and H and C from a sum that
/// involves `C` — behaviour that was undocumented until the community measured it,
/// and that the `ed a2` family of vector files pins.
pub fn ini_ind<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, inc: bool) {
    let v = bus.port_in(cpu.bc());
    bus.write(cpu.hl(), v);
    cpu.b = cpu.b.wrapping_sub(1);
    cpu.set_hl(if inc { cpu.hl().wrapping_add(1) } else { cpu.hl().wrapping_sub(1) });
    block_io_flags(cpu, v, if inc { cpu.c.wrapping_add(1) } else { cpu.c.wrapping_sub(1) });
}

/// `OUTI` / `OUTD`: write `(HL)` to a port, step `HL`, decrement `B`.
///
/// `B` is decremented **before** the port write, so the port address the write
/// lands on has the decremented `B` on its high half. That ordering is observable
/// in the `ports` array and is the difference between `outi` and a naive reading.
pub fn outi_outd<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, inc: bool) {
    let v = bus.read(cpu.hl());
    cpu.b = cpu.b.wrapping_sub(1);
    bus.port_out(cpu.bc(), v);
    cpu.set_hl(if inc { cpu.hl().wrapping_add(1) } else { cpu.hl().wrapping_sub(1) });
    block_io_flags(cpu, v, cpu.l);
}

/// The block-I/O flag set, shared by all eight forms.
fn block_io_flags(cpu: &mut Z80, v: u8, addend: u8) {
    let sum = u16::from(v) + u16::from(addend);
    cpu.f = flags::sz53(cpu.b)
        | if v & 0x80 != 0 { N } else { 0 }
        | if sum > 0xFF { H | C } else { 0 }
        | if flags::parity(((sum & 7) as u8) ^ cpu.b) { PV } else { 0 };
    cpu.q = 1;
    let _ = (S, Z); // named in the docs above; the bits come via sz53.
}
```

**Note:** the trailing `let _ = (S, Z);` is dead weight — remove the `S, Z` from
the `use` line instead if clippy flags them as unused. Do not leave a discard
statement in shipped code to silence a warning.

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the block transfers in `ops/load.rs`**

```rust
/// `LDI` / `LDD`: move `(HL)` to `(DE)`, step both, decrement `BC`.
///
/// The flags are unlike anything else on the chip: P/V is **`BC != 0`**, a repeat
/// count rather than a parity or an overflow, and F3/F5 come from `A + the byte
/// moved` — bit 3 into F3 and **bit 1** into F5, from a sum that appears in no
/// register. Carry is preserved.
pub fn ldi_ldd<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, inc: bool) {
    let v = bus.read(cpu.hl());
    bus.write(cpu.de(), v);
    let step = if inc { 1u16.wrapping_neg().wrapping_neg() } else { 1u16.wrapping_neg() };
    cpu.set_hl(cpu.hl().wrapping_add(step));
    cpu.set_de(cpu.de().wrapping_add(step));
    cpu.set_bc(cpu.bc().wrapping_sub(1));
    let n = cpu.a.wrapping_add(v);
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & (C | Z | S))
        | (n & F3)
        | if n & 0x02 != 0 { F5 } else { 0 }
        | if cpu.bc() != 0 { PV } else { 0 };
    cpu.q = 1;
}

/// `CPI` / `CPD`: compare `A` with `(HL)`, step `HL`, decrement `BC`.
///
/// `A` is not written and carry is preserved — the difference from `CP`. F3/F5 come
/// from `A - value - H`, by the same trick as [`ldi_ldd`].
pub fn cpi_cpd<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, inc: bool) {
    let v = bus.read(cpu.hl());
    let diff = cpu.a.wrapping_sub(v);
    let h = (cpu.a & 0x0F) < (v & 0x0F);
    let step = if inc { 1u16 } else { 1u16.wrapping_neg() };
    cpu.set_hl(cpu.hl().wrapping_add(step));
    cpu.set_bc(cpu.bc().wrapping_sub(1));
    let n = diff.wrapping_sub(u8::from(h));
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & C)
        | N
        | crate::flags::sz53(diff) & (S | Z)
        | if h { H } else { 0 }
        | (n & F3)
        | if n & 0x02 != 0 { F5 } else { 0 }
        | if cpu.bc() != 0 { PV } else { 0 };
    cpu.q = 1;
}
```

**Note on `ldi_ldd`'s `step`:** `1u16.wrapping_neg().wrapping_neg()` is a
convoluted way to write `1`. Write it plainly:

```rust
    let step: u16 = if inc { 1 } else { 0xFFFF };
```

`0xFFFF` added with `wrapping_add` is a decrement, which is what both branches
need and which reads as one operation rather than two.

- [ ] **Step 5: Add `adc16`, `sbc16` and `neg` to `ops/alu.rs`**

```rust
/// `ADC HL,rr`: the full flag set, unlike [`add16`].
///
/// S, Z and P/V are all written here and all *preserved* by `ADD HL,rr`. The same
/// operation on the same data with different flags, distinguished only by which
/// page the opcode is on.
#[must_use]
pub fn adc16(cpu: &mut Z80, a: u16, b: u16) -> u16 {
    let carry_in = u32::from(cpu.f & C != 0);
    let sum = u32::from(a) + u32::from(b) + carry_in;
    let r = sum as u16;
    let h = (a & 0x0FFF) + (b & 0x0FFF) + carry_in as u16 > 0x0FFF;
    let ovf = (a ^ b) & 0x8000 == 0 && (a ^ r) & 0x8000 != 0;
    cpu.f = (((r >> 8) as u8) & (S | F5 | F3))
        | if r == 0 { Z } else { 0 }
        | if h { H } else { 0 }
        | if ovf { PV } else { 0 }
        | if sum > 0xFFFF { C } else { 0 };
    cpu.q = 1;
    r
}

/// `SBC HL,rr`: as [`adc16`], with N set and the borrow rules.
#[must_use]
pub fn sbc16(cpu: &mut Z80, a: u16, b: u16) -> u16 {
    let borrow = i32::from(cpu.f & C != 0);
    let diff = i32::from(a) - i32::from(b) - borrow;
    let r = diff as u16;
    let h = i32::from(a & 0x0FFF) - i32::from(b & 0x0FFF) - borrow < 0;
    let ovf = (a ^ b) & 0x8000 != 0 && (a ^ r) & 0x8000 != 0;
    cpu.f = (((r >> 8) as u8) & (S | F5 | F3))
        | N
        | if r == 0 { Z } else { 0 }
        | if h { H } else { 0 }
        | if ovf { PV } else { 0 }
        | if diff < 0 { C } else { 0 };
    cpu.q = 1;
    r
}

/// `NEG`: `0 - A`, with subtraction flags.
///
/// P/V is set for `A = 0x80` and no other value: −128 has no positive counterpart
/// in eight bits. A single-value condition is the kind a core hard-codes to
/// `false` and never notices.
pub fn neg(cpu: &mut Z80) {
    let a = cpu.a;
    cpu.a = 0;
    sub(cpu, a, false);
}
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Write `RLD`/`RRD` in `ops/bits.rs`**

```rust
/// `RLD`: a twelve-bit left rotation through `A`'s low nibble and `(HL)`.
///
/// For packed BCD. `A`'s low nibble takes `(HL)`'s high nibble, `(HL)` shifts up,
/// and `A`'s old low nibble becomes `(HL)`'s low. Carry is preserved.
pub fn rld<B: crate::Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) {
    let m = bus.read(cpu.hl());
    let new_m = (m << 4) | (cpu.a & 0x0F);
    cpu.a = (cpu.a & 0xF0) | (m >> 4);
    bus.write(cpu.hl(), new_m);
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & C) | crate::flags::sz53p(cpu.a);
    cpu.q = 1;
}

/// `RRD`: [`rld`] mirrored.
pub fn rrd<B: crate::Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) {
    let m = bus.read(cpu.hl());
    let new_m = (m >> 4) | (cpu.a << 4);
    cpu.a = (cpu.a & 0xF0) | (m & 0x0F);
    bus.write(cpu.hl(), new_m);
    cpu.f = (cpu.f & C) | crate::flags::sz53p(cpu.a);
    cpu.q = 1;
}
```

- [ ] **Step 7: Write `ed_page` in `decode.rs`**

```rust
/// The `ED` page: 80 defined opcodes out of 256.
///
/// The undefined ones behave as two `NOP`s (the prefix having already cost its
/// M1), and upstream ships no file for them — so they are reachable and untested,
/// which is why the arm is explicit rather than a fallthrough.
pub fn ed_page<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B) -> u32 {
    let op = cpu.fetch(bus);
    match op {
        // IN r,(C) -- and 0x70 is `IN (C)`, which flags the byte and discards it.
        0x40 | 0x48 | 0x50 | 0x58 | 0x60 | 0x68 | 0x70 | 0x78 => {
            let v = io::in_r_c(cpu, bus);
            let dst = (op >> 3) & 7;
            if dst != 6 {
                set_reg(cpu, bus, dst, v);
            }
            12
        }
        // OUT (C),r -- 0x71 outputs zero on an NMOS Z80, which is the part that is
        // not guessable and is why the suite's generator options say NMOS.
        0x41 | 0x49 | 0x51 | 0x59 | 0x61 | 0x69 | 0x71 | 0x79 => {
            let src = (op >> 3) & 7;
            let v = if src == 6 { 0 } else { reg(cpu, bus, src) };
            io::out_c_r(cpu, bus, v);
            12
        }
        // SBC HL,rr and ADC HL,rr
        0x42 | 0x52 | 0x62 | 0x72 => {
            let r = alu::sbc16(cpu, cpu.hl(), rp(cpu, (op >> 4) & 3));
            cpu.set_hl(r);
            15
        }
        0x4A | 0x5A | 0x6A | 0x7A => {
            let r = alu::adc16(cpu, cpu.hl(), rp(cpu, (op >> 4) & 3));
            cpu.set_hl(r);
            15
        }
        // LD (nn),rr and LD rr,(nn)
        0x43 | 0x53 | 0x63 | 0x73 => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            let v = rp(cpu, (op >> 4) & 3);
            bus.write(nn, v as u8);
            bus.write(nn.wrapping_add(1), (v >> 8) as u8);
            cpu.q = 0;
            20
        }
        0x4B | 0x5B | 0x6B | 0x7B => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            let lo = bus.read(nn);
            let hi = bus.read(nn.wrapping_add(1));
            set_rp(cpu, (op >> 4) & 3, u16::from(hi) << 8 | u16::from(lo));
            cpu.q = 0;
            20
        }
        0x44 | 0x4C | 0x54 | 0x5C | 0x64 | 0x6C | 0x74 | 0x7C => {
            alu::neg(cpu);
            8
        }
        // RETN and RETI: a return that restores IFF1 from IFF2.
        0x45 | 0x4D | 0x55 | 0x5D | 0x65 | 0x6D | 0x75 | 0x7D => {
            cpu.iff1 = cpu.iff2;
            flow::ret(cpu, bus);
            cpu.q = 0;
            14
        }
        // IM 0 / IM 1 / IM 2, each with duplicate encodings.
        0x46 | 0x4E | 0x66 | 0x6E => { cpu.im = 0; cpu.q = 0; 8 }
        0x56 | 0x76 => { cpu.im = 1; cpu.q = 0; 8 }
        0x5E | 0x7E => { cpu.im = 2; cpu.q = 0; 8 }
        0x47 => { cpu.i = cpu.a; cpu.q = 0; 9 }
        0x4F => { cpu.r = cpu.a; cpu.q = 0; 9 }
        // LD A,I and LD A,R: P/V leaks IFF2, and `p` marks the instruction because
        // an interrupt landing here corrupts that flag on real hardware.
        0x57 | 0x5F => {
            cpu.a = if op == 0x57 { cpu.i } else { cpu.r };
            cpu.f = (cpu.f & C)
                | flags::sz53(cpu.a)
                | if cpu.iff2 { flags::PV } else { 0 };
            cpu.q = 1;
            cpu.p = 1;
            9
        }
        0x67 => { bits::rrd(cpu, bus); 18 }
        0x6F => { bits::rld(cpu, bus); 18 }
        // The block instructions. The repeating forms rewind PC by two rather than
        // looping, so each iteration is one `step` and an interrupt can land
        // between them -- as it can on the chip.
        0xA0 | 0xA8 | 0xB0 | 0xB8 => {
            let inc = op & 0x08 == 0;
            load::ldi_ldd(cpu, bus, inc);
            if op >= 0xB0 && cpu.bc() != 0 {
                cpu.pc = cpu.pc.wrapping_sub(2);
                21
            } else {
                16
            }
        }
        0xA1 | 0xA9 | 0xB1 | 0xB9 => {
            let inc = op & 0x08 == 0;
            load::cpi_cpd(cpu, bus, inc);
            // CPIR/CPDR stop early on a match, which LDIR has no equivalent of.
            if op >= 0xB1 && cpu.bc() != 0 && cpu.f & flags::Z == 0 {
                cpu.pc = cpu.pc.wrapping_sub(2);
                21
            } else {
                16
            }
        }
        0xA2 | 0xAA | 0xB2 | 0xBA => {
            io::ini_ind(cpu, bus, op & 0x08 == 0);
            if op >= 0xB2 && cpu.b != 0 {
                cpu.pc = cpu.pc.wrapping_sub(2);
                21
            } else {
                16
            }
        }
        0xA3 | 0xAB | 0xB3 | 0xBB => {
            io::outi_outd(cpu, bus, op & 0x08 == 0);
            if op >= 0xB3 && cpu.b != 0 {
                cpu.pc = cpu.pc.wrapping_sub(2);
                21
            } else {
                16
            }
        }
        // The undefined opcodes: two NOPs' worth of time, no state change beyond
        // the two M1 cycles already charged to `R`.
        _ => {
            cpu.q = 0;
            8
        }
    }
}
```

and the base-page arm `0xED => ed_page(cpu, bus),`.

- [ ] **Step 8: Run the tests, the gate, and commit**

```bash
cargo test -p z80 --lib
cargo fmt --all && cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "feat(z80): the ED page -- block moves, block I/O, 16-bit ADC/SBC

LDIR rewinds PC by two rather than looping inside step. A loop would make
a 65,536-byte LDIR one uninterruptible step, which is wrong on the chip
and unverifiable against single-instruction vectors.

The flag rules here exist nowhere else: LDI's P/V is BC != 0 -- a repeat
count, not a parity or an overflow -- and its F3/F5 come from A plus the
byte moved, bit 3 to F3 and bit 1 to F5, from a sum held in no register.

ADC HL,rr writes S, Z and P/V where ADD HL,rr preserves them. Same
operation, same data, different flags, distinguished only by which page
the opcode sits on. IN r,(C) flags its byte and IN A,(n) does not.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 11: The `DD` and `FD` index pages

**Files:**
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/decode.rs` (add `index_page`)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/cpu.rs` (add the `disp` helper)

**Interfaces:**
- Produces: `decode::index_page(cpu, bus, use_iy) -> u32`, `Z80::disp`.

### The 252 files per page, and what a prefix actually does

`DD` does not define 252 new instructions. It sets a rule for the *next*
instruction: **wherever that instruction would have used `HL`, it uses `IX`
instead, and wherever it would have used `(HL)`, it uses `(IX+d)` with a signed
displacement byte read after the opcode.** `FD` is the same rule with `IY`.

Three consequences, all of them things a core gets wrong:

1. `H` and `L` become `IXH` and `IXL` — the *halves* of `IX`. So `DD 44` is
   `LD B,IXH`, a real instruction with a real vector file. The prefix rewrites
   register operands, not just memory ones.
2. **But not when the other operand is `(IX+d)`.** `DD 66` is `LD H,(IX+d)`:
   the destination is plain `H`, not `IXH`. The displaced-memory form suppresses
   the register rewrite on the *other* side, because a single instruction cannot
   both use `IX` as a pointer and as the register file. `DD 74` is
   `LD (IX+d),H` for the same reason.
3. An opcode with no `HL` in it is **unaffected**, and upstream ships a file for
   it anyway: `DD 00` is `NOP` that took an extra 4 T-states for the prefix. So
   the fallthrough arm is a real, tested code path — not an error case.

`EX DE,HL` is the exception that proves the rule is about *encoding*, not about
the letters in the mnemonic: `DD EB` still exchanges `DE` with **`HL`**, never
with `IX`. The instruction reaches `HL` by a path the prefix does not intercept.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// `DD` makes `H` and `L` the halves of `IX`.
    ///
    /// `DD 44` is `LD B,IXH`. The prefix rewrites *register* operands, not only
    /// memory ones — the part a core that treats `DD` as "add a displacement"
    /// misses entirely.
    #[test]
    fn a_dd_prefix_turns_h_and_l_into_the_halves_of_ix() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x1234;
        c.h = 0x99;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x44]); // LD B,IXH
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8);
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0x12, "IXH, not H");

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.iy = 0x1234;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xFD, 0x4D]); // LD C,IYL
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.c, 0x34, "and FD reaches IY's halves");
    }

    /// But a displaced-memory operand suppresses the rewrite on the other side.
    ///
    /// `DD 66` is `LD H,(IX+d)` — plain `H`, not `IXH`. One instruction cannot use
    /// `IX` as both a pointer and a register half, so the memory form wins and the
    /// register stays unprefixed. A core that rewrote both would load `IXH` here and
    /// pass every non-displaced test.
    #[test]
    fn a_displaced_operand_leaves_the_other_register_unprefixed() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.h = 0x11;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x66, 0x05]); // LD H,(IX+5)
        m.ram[0x2005] = 0x7E;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 19);
        assert_eq!(c.h, 0x7E, "H, not IXH");
        assert_eq!(c.ix, 0x2000, "and IX is untouched");

        // And the store direction: LD (IX+d),H writes plain H.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.h = 0x42;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x74, 0x03]);
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 19);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2003], 0x42, "H, not IXH");
    }

    /// The displacement is **signed**.
    ///
    /// `0xFB` is −5, so `(IX-5)`. An unsigned read would address `IX+251` — off by
    /// 256, which lands inside a nearby structure rather than crashing, and is
    /// therefore the kind of bug that shows up as garbled sprites.
    #[test]
    fn the_displacement_is_a_signed_byte() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2010;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x7E, 0xFB]); // LD A,(IX-5)
        m.ram[0x200B] = 0xAB;
        m.ram[0x210B] = 0xCD; // where an unsigned read would land
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 19);
        assert_eq!(c.a, 0xAB, "IX-5, not IX+251");
    }

    /// `EX DE,HL` under a prefix still exchanges `HL`.
    ///
    /// The exception that shows the rule is about encoding rather than mnemonics:
    /// `DD EB` reaches `HL` by a path the prefix does not intercept. A core that
    /// substituted on the mnemonic would exchange `DE` with `IX` and be wrong in a
    /// way no non-prefixed test can see.
    #[test]
    fn ex_de_hl_under_a_prefix_still_touches_hl() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.set_de(0xAAAA);
        c.set_hl(0xBBBB);
        c.ix = 0xCCCC;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xEB]);
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.de(), 0xBBBB);
        assert_eq!(c.hl(), 0xAAAA);
        assert_eq!(c.ix, 0xCCCC, "IX is not involved");
    }

    /// A prefix on an opcode with no `HL` costs 4 extra T-states and does nothing.
    ///
    /// `DD 00` is a tested opcode with its own vector file, not an error case. So the
    /// fallthrough is a real code path and must charge the prefix's M1 — which it
    /// does by construction, since the prefix fetch already bumped `R`.
    #[test]
    fn a_prefix_on_an_unaffected_opcode_only_costs_time() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x00]);
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8, "4 for the prefix, 4 for the NOP");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x102);
        assert_eq!(c.r, 2, "two M1 cycles, so R advanced twice");
    }

    /// `INC (IX+d)` reads, modifies, writes — and preserves carry, as `INC` does.
    #[test]
    fn inc_of_displaced_memory_is_a_read_modify_write_at_twenty_three_tstates() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.f = C;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x34, 0x02]);
        m.ram[0x2002] = 0x7F;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 23);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2002], 0x80);
        assert_eq!(c.f & PV, PV, "0x7F to 0x80 overflows");
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C, "and INC never touches carry");
    }

    /// `ADD IX,IX` doubles it, and `ADD IX,rr` uses the prefixed register.
    #[test]
    fn add_ix_uses_ix_on_both_sides_where_the_encoding_says_hl() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x1111;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x29]); // ADD IX,IX
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 15);
        assert_eq!(c.ix, 0x2222);

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x0001;
        c.set_bc(0x0002);
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0x09]); // ADD IX,BC
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.ix, 0x0003, "BC stays BC; only the destination is prefixed");
    }

    /// `LD SP,IX` and `JP (IX)` and `EX (SP),IX` and `PUSH IX`.
    ///
    /// Four instructions that read `HL` in the unprefixed encoding and must all
    /// follow the substitution. `LD SP,HL` is the one most often missed, because it
    /// is the only 16-bit register-to-register move on the chip.
    #[test]
    fn the_sixteen_bit_hl_instructions_all_substitute() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x1234;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xF9]); // LD SP,IX
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 10);
        assert_eq!(c.sp, 0x1234);

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x4321;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xE9]); // JP (IX)
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 8);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x4321);

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.ix = 0xBEEF;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xE5]); // PUSH IX
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 15);
        assert_eq!(c.sp, 0x2FFE);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFF], 0xBE, "the high byte goes first");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFE], 0xEF);
    }

    /// A prefix followed by another prefix: the second one wins.
    ///
    /// `DD FD 21 nn nn` loads `IY`, not `IX`. Each prefix restarts the rule, and the
    /// suite has a file for `dd fd` behaviour by way of every `fd NN` file being
    /// reachable through it. The natural implementation — a loop that re-dispatches
    /// on the prefix — gets this for free; a match arm that assumed the next byte is
    /// an opcode does not.
    #[test]
    fn the_last_prefix_before_the_opcode_is_the_one_that_applies() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xFD, 0x21, 0x34, 0x12]);
        let t = c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.iy, 0x1234, "the FD won");
        assert_eq!(c.ix, 0x0000, "and the DD did nothing but cost time");
        assert_eq!(t, 18, "4 for the discarded DD, then a 14-T-state LD IY,nn");
        assert_eq!(c.r, 3, "three M1 cycles: DD, FD, 21");
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Add `disp` to `cpu.rs`**

```rust
    /// Reads the signed displacement byte of an `(IX+d)` operand.
    ///
    /// Signed: `0xFB` is −5, giving `IX-5`. Reading it unsigned would address
    /// `IX+251` — off by 256, which lands in whatever is nearby rather than
    /// faulting, and shows up as garbled graphics rather than a crash.
    ///
    /// This is an operand read, not an M1 cycle, so `R` is **not** bumped.
    pub fn disp<B: Bus>(&mut self, bus: &mut B) -> i8 {
        self.imm(bus) as i8
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write `index_page` in `decode.rs`**

```rust
/// The `DD` and `FD` pages: 252 opcodes each.
///
/// A prefix is a rule for the *next* instruction, not 252 new instructions:
/// wherever it would use `HL`, use `IX`; wherever `(HL)`, use `(IX+d)` with a
/// signed displacement read after the opcode.
///
/// Two rules that fall out of that and are easy to get wrong:
///
/// - `H` and `L` become `IXH` and `IXL`, so `DD 44` is `LD B,IXH` — a real
///   instruction with a real vector file. The prefix rewrites register operands,
///   not just memory ones.
/// - **Except** when the other operand is `(IX+d)`: `DD 66` is `LD H,(IX+d)` with
///   a plain `H`, because one instruction cannot use `IX` as both a pointer and a
///   register half.
///
/// `EX DE,HL` is untouched — it reaches `HL` by a path the encoding does not route
/// through this substitution. And an opcode with no `HL` in it simply costs 4 extra
/// T-states, which is a tested path (`DD 00`), not an error.
pub fn index_page<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, use_iy: bool) -> u32 {
    let op = cpu.fetch(bus);

    // A prefix restarts the rule: DD FD 21 loads IY. Re-dispatching here is what
    // makes that fall out rather than needing a special case.
    match op {
        0xDD => return 4 + index_page(cpu, bus, false),
        0xFD => return 4 + index_page(cpu, bus, true),
        0xCB => return 4 + index_cb_page(cpu, bus, use_iy),
        0xED => return 4 + ed_page(cpu, bus),
        _ => {}
    }

    let idx = if use_iy { cpu.iy } else { cpu.ix };

    /// Reads register `n` with `H`/`L` rewritten to the index halves, but with 6
    /// meaning `(idx+d)`.
    macro_rules! ireg {
        ($n:expr, $d:expr) => {
            match $n {
                4 => (idx >> 8) as u8,
                5 => idx as u8,
                6 => bus.read($d),
                n => reg(cpu, bus, n),
            }
        };
    }

    match op {
        // The 8-bit loads. Destination 6 or source 6 means displaced memory, and
        // that form suppresses the H/L rewrite on the other side.
        0x40..=0x7F if op != 0x76 => {
            let dst = (op >> 3) & 7;
            let src = op & 7;
            if dst == 6 {
                let d = cpu.disp(bus);
                let addr = idx.wrapping_add(d as u16);
                // Plain `reg`: the source keeps H and L, not IXH and IXL.
                let v = reg(cpu, bus, src);
                bus.write(addr, v);
                cpu.q = 0;
                19
            } else if src == 6 {
                let d = cpu.disp(bus);
                let addr = idx.wrapping_add(d as u16);
                let v = bus.read(addr);
                // Plain `set_reg`, for the same reason.
                set_reg(cpu, bus, dst, v);
                cpu.q = 0;
                19
            } else {
                let v = ireg!(src, 0);
                match dst {
                    4 => set_index(cpu, use_iy, (idx & 0x00FF) | u16::from(v) << 8),
                    5 => set_index(cpu, use_iy, (idx & 0xFF00) | u16::from(v)),
                    n => set_reg(cpu, bus, n, v),
                }
                cpu.q = 0;
                8
            }
        }
        // LD (IX+d),n -- two operands, and the displacement comes first.
        0x36 => {
            let d = cpu.disp(bus);
            let n = cpu.imm(bus);
            bus.write(idx.wrapping_add(d as u16), n);
            cpu.q = 0;
            19
        }
        // The 8-bit ALU against a register half or displaced memory.
        0x80..=0xBF => {
            let src = op & 7;
            let (v, t) = if src == 6 {
                let d = cpu.disp(bus);
                (bus.read(idx.wrapping_add(d as u16)), 19)
            } else {
                (ireg!(src, 0), 8)
            };
            alu_op(cpu, (op >> 3) & 7, v);
            t
        }
        // INC/DEC of a half or of displaced memory.
        0x34 | 0x35 => {
            let d = cpu.disp(bus);
            let addr = idx.wrapping_add(d as u16);
            let v = bus.read(addr);
            let r = if op == 0x34 { alu::inc8(cpu, v) } else { alu::dec8(cpu, v) };
            bus.write(addr, r);
            23
        }
        0x24 | 0x25 | 0x2C | 0x2D => {
            let high = op < 0x2C;
            let v = if high { (idx >> 8) as u8 } else { idx as u8 };
            let r = if op & 1 == 0 { alu::inc8(cpu, v) } else { alu::dec8(cpu, v) };
            set_index(
                cpu,
                use_iy,
                if high { (idx & 0x00FF) | u16::from(r) << 8 } else { (idx & 0xFF00) | u16::from(r) },
            );
            8
        }
        // The 16-bit forms: ADD IX,rr with rr=2 meaning IX itself.
        0x09 | 0x19 | 0x29 | 0x39 => {
            let which = (op >> 4) & 3;
            let operand = if which == 2 { idx } else { rp(cpu, which) };
            let r = alu::add16(cpu, idx, operand);
            set_index(cpu, use_iy, r);
            15
        }
        0x21 => { let nn = cpu.imm16(bus); set_index(cpu, use_iy, nn); cpu.q = 0; 14 }
        0x22 => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            bus.write(nn, idx as u8);
            bus.write(nn.wrapping_add(1), (idx >> 8) as u8);
            cpu.q = 0;
            20
        }
        0x2A => {
            let nn = cpu.imm16(bus);
            let lo = bus.read(nn);
            let hi = bus.read(nn.wrapping_add(1));
            set_index(cpu, use_iy, u16::from(hi) << 8 | u16::from(lo));
            cpu.q = 0;
            20
        }
        0x23 => { set_index(cpu, use_iy, idx.wrapping_add(1)); cpu.q = 0; 10 }
        0x2B => { set_index(cpu, use_iy, idx.wrapping_sub(1)); cpu.q = 0; 10 }
        0xE1 => { let v = load::pop(cpu, bus); set_index(cpu, use_iy, v); cpu.q = 0; 14 }
        0xE5 => { load::push(cpu, bus, idx); cpu.q = 0; 15 }
        0xE3 => {
            let v = load::pop(cpu, bus);
            load::push(cpu, bus, idx);
            set_index(cpu, use_iy, v);
            cpu.q = 0;
            23
        }
        0xE9 => { cpu.pc = idx; cpu.q = 0; 8 }
        0xF9 => { cpu.sp = idx; cpu.q = 0; 10 }
        // Everything else: the prefix cost 4 T-states and changed nothing. A real,
        // tested path -- `dd 00.json` exists -- not an error case.
        _ => 4 + base(cpu, bus, op),
    }
}

/// Writes `v` to `IX` or `IY`.
fn set_index(cpu: &mut Z80, use_iy: bool, v: u16) {
    if use_iy {
        cpu.iy = v;
    } else {
        cpu.ix = v;
    }
}
```

**Note on the fallthrough arm:** it calls `base(cpu, bus, op)` — the base-page
dispatcher **with the opcode already fetched**, not `step`. Task 5's `step` must
therefore be split into `step` (fetch, then dispatch) and
`base(cpu, bus, op) -> u32` (dispatch an already-fetched opcode), so that this arm
does not re-fetch. If Task 5 wrote a monolithic `step`, refactor it here as the
first step of Step 4 — extracting a function is a smaller change than the
alternative, which is duplicating the base page's 250 arms.

**Note on the `0x40..=0x7F` guard:** `op != 0x76` excludes `HALT`, which sits in
the middle of the load block. `DD 76` is `HALT` after a wasted prefix, so it falls
through to the last arm and costs `4 + 4`.

- [ ] **Step 5: Wire the prefixes into `base`**

```rust
        0xDD => index_page(cpu, bus, false),
        0xFD => index_page(cpu, bus, true),
```


- [ ] **Step 7: The gate, and commit**

```bash
cargo fmt --all && cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "feat(z80): the DD and FD index pages

A prefix is a rule for the next instruction, not 252 instructions: use IX
where the encoding says HL, and (IX+d) where it says (HL).

Two rules fall out of that and are the ones cores miss. H and L become
IXH and IXL, so DD 44 is LD B,IXH -- the prefix rewrites register
operands, not only memory ones. But a displaced operand suppresses that
rewrite on the other side: DD 66 is LD H,(IX+d) with a plain H, because
one instruction cannot use IX as a pointer and a register half at once.

EX DE,HL is untouched: it reaches HL by a path the encoding does not
route through the substitution. DD 00 is a tested opcode that costs 4
extra T-states, so the fallthrough is a real path rather than an error.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 12: The `DD CB` and `FD CB` double-prefix pages

**Files:**
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/decode.rs` (add `index_cb_page`)

**Interfaces:**
- Produces: `decode::index_cb_page(cpu, bus, use_iy) -> u32`.

### Why these 512 files are the hardest part of the chip

`DD CB d op` is where the Z80's encoding stops being systematic:

1. **The displacement comes before the opcode.** The byte order is prefix,
   `CB`, *displacement*, opcode — so the operand is read before the instruction
   is known. Every other multi-byte instruction on the chip reads its opcode
   first.
2. **The register field is ignored for the operand and used for a second
   destination.** `DD CB 05 06` is `RLC (IX+5)`, and `DD CB 05 00` is *also*
   `RLC (IX+5)` — but it additionally copies the result into `B`. All eight
   register encodings address the same memory; seven of them also write a
   register. These are the undocumented forms, and upstream ships a file for
   every one, which is why the plan covers all 256 rather than the 32 that
   Zilog documents.
3. **`BIT` has no destination**, so its eight encodings are genuinely identical
   and none of them writes a register.
4. **`R` advances twice, not four times.** `DD` and `CB` are M1 cycles; the
   displacement and the final opcode byte are not. A core that bumped `R` on
   every byte would be wrong by two here — and `R` is compared by the suite.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// The displacement precedes the opcode.
    ///
    /// prefix, CB, **displacement**, opcode — the operand is read before the
    /// instruction is known. Every other multi-byte instruction reads its opcode
    /// first, so this is the one place the natural decode order is wrong.
    #[test]
    fn the_displacement_comes_before_the_opcode_byte() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        // DD CB 05 06 = RLC (IX+5)
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x05, 0x06]);
        m.ram[0x2005] = 0x81;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 23);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2005], 0x03, "0x81 rotated left is 0x03");
        assert_eq!(c.f & C, C, "with bit 7 into carry");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x104, "and all four bytes consumed");
    }

    /// The register field addresses no operand and names a second destination.
    ///
    /// `DD CB 05 00` is `RLC (IX+5)` **and** `LD B,result`. All eight encodings
    /// address the same memory; seven also write a register. Undocumented, and
    /// upstream ships all 256 files — which is why this page is 256 and not 32.
    #[test]
    fn the_register_field_names_an_extra_destination_not_the_operand() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.b = 0xFF;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x05, 0x00]);
        m.ram[0x2005] = 0x81;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 23);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2005], 0x03, "memory still gets the result");
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0x03, "and so does B");

        // The documented form (field 6) writes memory only.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.b = 0xFF;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x05, 0x06]);
        m.ram[0x2005] = 0x81;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.b, 0xFF, "field 6 leaves the registers alone");
    }

    /// `SET` and `RES` copy to the register too; `BIT` has nothing to copy.
    ///
    /// `BIT` produces no value, so its eight encodings really are identical and none
    /// writes a register. A core that applied the copy uniformly would clobber a
    /// register on every `BIT (IX+d)` — and `BIT` is the one form software uses in
    /// hot loops.
    #[test]
    fn set_and_res_copy_to_the_register_but_bit_writes_nothing() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.d = 0x00;
        // DD CB 00 C2 = SET 0,(IX+0), also into D
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0xC2]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x00;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 23);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2000], 0x01);
        assert_eq!(c.d, 0x01, "SET copies to the named register");

        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        c.d = 0x77;
        // DD CB 00 42 = BIT 0,(IX+0) -- the field is 2 (D) and must be ignored
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0x42]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x01;
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 20, "BIT is 20, not 23 -- no write cycle");
        assert_eq!(c.d, 0x77, "and D is untouched");
        assert_eq!(c.f & Z, 0, "bit 0 was set");
    }

    /// `R` advances twice: `DD` and `CB` are M1 cycles, the other two bytes are not.
    ///
    /// Four bytes, two M1 cycles. A core that bumped `R` per byte would be off by
    /// two — and the suite compares `R`, so this is caught rather than mysterious.
    #[test]
    fn the_double_prefix_bumps_the_refresh_counter_exactly_twice() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.r = 0;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0x06]);
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.r, 2, "two M1 cycles across four bytes");
    }

    /// `BIT b,(IX+d)`'s undocumented flags come from the **address**, not the byte.
    ///
    /// F3/F5 are bits 11 and 13 of `IX+d` — the internal address latch leaking onto
    /// the flag register. For `BIT b,(HL)` on the plain `CB` page they come from
    /// `WZ`; here the displaced address is what `WZ` holds.
    ///
    /// Hand-computed, and the two addresses are chosen to disagree so a swapped pair
    /// of bits fails rather than passing twice: `0x0800` has bit 11 set and bit 13
    /// clear (F3 set, F5 clear), and `0x2000` is its mirror. An address like `0x2A00`
    /// would be useless here — it has *both* bits set, so it cannot tell the two
    /// flags apart.
    #[test]
    fn bit_of_displaced_memory_takes_f3_and_f5_from_the_address() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x0800;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0x46]);
        m.ram[0x0800] = 0xFF;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.wz, 0x0800, "the latch holds the displaced address");
        assert_eq!(c.f & F3, F3, "bit 11 of 0x0800 is set");
        assert_eq!(c.f & F5, 0, "bit 13 is clear");

        // 0x2000: bit 13 set, bit 11 clear -- the mirror case.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0x46]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0xFF;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.f & F5, F5);
        assert_eq!(c.f & F3, 0);
    }

    /// `SLL (IX+d)` exists and shifts a 1 into bit 0.
    #[test]
    fn sll_of_displaced_memory_shifts_a_one_in() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.ix = 0x2000;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x00, 0x36]);
        m.ram[0x2000] = 0x00;
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2000], 0x01, "a 1, not a 0 -- that is what makes it SLL");
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `index_cb_page`**

```rust
/// The `DD CB` and `FD CB` pages: 256 opcodes each, all of them on displaced memory.
///
/// The one place the Z80's encoding stops being systematic:
///
/// - **The displacement precedes the opcode** (prefix, `CB`, displacement, opcode),
///   so the operand address is known before the operation is.
/// - **The register field does not select the operand.** All eight encodings act on
///   `(idx+d)`; seven of them *also* copy the result into the named register. Those
///   seven are undocumented and upstream ships a file for each, which is why this
///   page is 256 forms rather than the 32 Zilog lists.
/// - **`BIT` produces no value**, so its eight encodings are genuinely identical and
///   none writes a register. It also costs 20 rather than 23: no write cycle.
/// - `R` advances **twice** across the four bytes. Only the prefix and the `CB` are
///   M1 cycles.
pub fn index_cb_page<B: Bus>(cpu: &mut Z80, bus: &mut B, use_iy: bool) -> u32 {
    let d = cpu.disp(bus);
    // `imm`, not `fetch`: the final byte is not an M1 cycle and must not bump `R`.
    let op = cpu.imm(bus);

    let idx = if use_iy { cpu.iy } else { cpu.ix };
    let addr = idx.wrapping_add(d as u16);
    // The latch holds the displaced address, which is where BIT's F3/F5 come from.
    cpu.wz = addr;

    let v = bus.read(addr);
    let field = op & 7;
    let bit = (op >> 3) & 7;

    match op >> 6 {
        // The rotate and shift block.
        0 => {
            let r = bits::rot(cpu, bits::RotOp::from_index(bit), v);
            bus.write(addr, r);
            if field != 6 {
                set_reg(cpu, bus, field, r);
            }
            23
        }
        // BIT: no result, no write, no register copy, and 20 T-states.
        1 => {
            bits::bit_test(cpu, v, bit);
            // F3/F5 come from the address latch, not from the byte tested.
            cpu.f = (cpu.f & !(flags::F3 | flags::F5)) | ((cpu.wz >> 8) as u8 & (flags::F3 | flags::F5));
            20
        }
        // RES and SET.
        _ => {
            let r = if op >> 6 == 2 { v & !(1 << bit) } else { v | (1 << bit) };
            bus.write(addr, r);
            if field != 6 {
                set_reg(cpu, bus, field, r);
            }
            cpu.q = 0;
            23
        }
    }
}
```

**Note on the `set_reg` call:** `field` is never 6 in the branches that call it
(the `if` excludes it), so `set_reg` never takes the `(HL)` path and never issues a
stray memory write. Keep the guard; do not "simplify" it to an unconditional call.

**Note on `BIT`'s F3/F5:** `(cpu.wz >> 8) as u8` takes bits 8–15 of the address, so
`F3` (bit 3 of that byte) is address bit 11 and `F5` is address bit 13 — which is
what the test asserts with `0x0800` and `0x2000`. Do not shorten this to
`cpu.wz as u8`; that would read bits 3 and 5, and would pass whenever the low byte
happens to agree.

```bash
The one place the Z80's encoding stops being systematic. The
displacement comes *before* the opcode, so the operand address is known
before the operation is. The register field selects no operand: all
eight encodings act on (idx+d), and seven also copy the result into the
named register. Those seven are undocumented and upstream ships a file
for each, which is why this page is 256 forms and not the 32 Zilog
lists.

BIT produces no value, so its eight encodings are identical, none writes
a register, and it costs 20 rather than 23 -- there is no write cycle.
Its F3/F5 come from bits 11 and 13 of the displaced address, via the
internal latch, not from the byte tested.

R advances twice across the four bytes: only the prefix and the CB are
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Close the base page's development catch-all**

Task 5 left `other => unimplemented!("base opcode {other:#04X}")` at the end of
`base`, documented as a development-only panic. Every one of the 256 base opcodes is
now implemented, so that arm is dead — and leaving it would violate the Global
Constraint that the core never panics on guest input. A `#[deny]`-clean build does
not catch it, because the compiler cannot know the ranges are exhaustive.

Delete the arm and prove exhaustiveness by making the compiler check it:

```rust
        // Every one of the 256 base opcodes is implemented. The match is exhaustive
        // over `u8` by construction, so there is no catch-all -- and adding one back
        // would let a future gap in the ranges compile to a silent wrong answer
        // instead of a build failure.
```

If the compiler reports the match as non-exhaustive, **that is the point**: it is
naming a real gap in the ranges above. Find and implement the missing opcode. Do not
restore a catch-all to make the build pass.

```bash
cargo test -p z80 --lib
grep -rn 'unimplemented!\|todo!' crates/z80/src   # expect: no output
```

```bash
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "refactor(z80): no catch-all arm -- all 256 base opcodes exist

Task 5 left a development-only unimplemented! at the end of base. Every
base opcode is now implemented, so the arm is dead, and leaving it would
break the constraint that the core never panics on guest input.

Deleting it makes the compiler check exhaustiveness over u8. A gap in the
ranges is now a build failure rather than a silent wrong answer, which is
the whole reason not to restore a catch-all to quiet one.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```
