## Task 13: Interrupts, NMI, and the `EI` delay

**Files:**
- Create: `crates/z80/src/interrupt.rs`
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/lib.rs` (module + re-export)
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/cpu.rs` (`step` calls the acceptance check)

**Interfaces:**
- Produces: `Z80::{irq, nmi, ack_irq, ack_nmi}`, `Z80::pending_ei`.

### Why this task has no vectors and needs its own tests

Every vector file is **one instruction with no interrupt**. The suite pins the
`ei`, `iff1`, `iff2` and `im` *fields*, so a core that stores them wrongly is
caught — but nothing in 1,604,000 cases ever accepts an interrupt. This task's
correctness rests entirely on hand-written tests and on the mutation set in
Task 16. That is a statement about the suite's scope, not a licence to be vague:
the rules below come from the Zilog manual and are each pinned by a test that
would fail if the rule were dropped.

The rules:

- **`EI` does not enable interrupts.** It arms them for *after the next
  instruction*, so `EI` followed immediately by `RET` cannot be interrupted
  between them. This exists so an interrupt service routine can end
  `EI; RETI` without re-entering itself on the stack. The suite's `ei` field is
  exactly this pending flag.
- **`DI` takes effect immediately.** The asymmetry is deliberate on the chip and
  is the difference between a working ISR and a stack overflow.
- **NMI ignores `IFF1`** — it is non-maskable — and saves `IFF1` into `IFF2`,
  then clears `IFF1`. `RETN` restores it. That copy is the entire mechanism by
  which an NMI does not permanently disable interrupts.
- **A maskable interrupt clears both `IFF1` and `IFF2`**, so the ISR runs with
  interrupts off until it says otherwise.
- **`HALT` is exited by acceptance**, and `PC` must be advanced past the `HALT`
  before the return address is pushed — otherwise the ISR returns into the
  `HALT` and the machine wedges. This is the single most consequential
  off-by-one in the whole task.
- **Mode 2** builds the vector address as `I << 8 | (bus_value & 0xFE)`. The low
  bit is **masked**: the table is 16-bit entries, so an odd bus value would read
  a misaligned pair. CPS-1's sound board uses mode 1, but mode 2 is what the
  suite's `im` field can hold and what a wrong mask would silently break.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// `EI` arms interrupts for *after* the next instruction.
    ///
    /// So `EI; RET` cannot be interrupted between the two — which is why an ISR can
    /// end `EI; RETI` without re-entering itself. Getting this wrong produces a stack
    /// overflow under load and nothing at all when idle.
    #[test]
    fn ei_does_not_let_an_interrupt_in_until_after_the_next_instruction() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 1;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xFB, 0x00, 0x00]); // EI; NOP; NOP
        c.step(&mut m); // EI
        assert_eq!(c.ei, 1, "armed, not enabled");
        c.irq = true;
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), None, "the instruction after EI is protected");
        c.step(&mut m); // NOP
        assert_eq!(c.ei, 0, "the arming is consumed");
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), Some(13), "and now the interrupt lands");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0038, "mode 1 vectors to 0x38");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFF], 0x01, "the return address is the second NOP");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFE], 0x02);
    }

    /// `DI` takes effect immediately — the asymmetry with `EI` is deliberate.
    #[test]
    fn di_disables_immediately_where_ei_defers() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.iff2 = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0xF3]);
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert!(!c.iff1);
        assert!(!c.iff2);
        c.irq = true;
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), None, "no deferral on the way down");
    }

    /// A maskable interrupt clears both flip-flops.
    #[test]
    fn accepting_an_interrupt_clears_both_interrupt_flip_flops() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 1;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.iff2 = true;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), Some(13));
        assert!(!c.iff1, "the ISR runs with interrupts off");
        assert!(!c.iff2);
    }

    /// NMI ignores `IFF1`, saves it into `IFF2`, and vectors to 0x66.
    ///
    /// The save is the whole mechanism by which an NMI does not permanently disable
    /// interrupts: `RETN` copies it back. A core that skipped the copy would run
    /// correctly until the first NMI and then never take another maskable interrupt.
    #[test]
    fn nmi_ignores_iff1_but_saves_it_for_retn() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.iff2 = false;
        c.nmi = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        assert_eq!(c.ack_nmi(&mut m), Some(11));
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0066);
        assert!(!c.iff1, "cleared on entry");
        assert!(c.iff2, "and the old value saved here");
        assert!(!c.nmi, "the edge is consumed");

        // RETN puts it back.
        let mut m2 = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x0066, &[0xED, 0x45]);
        m2.ram[0x2FFF] = 0x02;
        m2.ram[0x2FFE] = 0x00;
        c.step(&mut m2);
        assert!(c.iff1, "RETN restores IFF1 from IFF2");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0200);
    }

    /// NMI outranks a simultaneous maskable interrupt.
    #[test]
    fn an_nmi_wins_against_a_simultaneous_maskable_interrupt() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 1;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.nmi = true;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        c.service(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0066, "0x66, not 0x38");
        assert!(c.irq, "and the maskable request is still pending");
    }

    /// Acceptance leaves `HALT`, and `PC` is past the `HALT` before the push.
    ///
    /// The consequential off-by-one of this whole task: push the `HALT`'s own address
    /// and the ISR returns into it, halting again forever. The machine would boot,
    /// run, and then freeze the first time an interrupt arrived during an idle loop —
    /// which is where a sound CPU spends most of its time.
    #[test]
    fn an_interrupt_leaves_halt_with_pc_past_the_halt_instruction() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x100;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 1;
        c.iff1 = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0x76]); // HALT
        c.step(&mut m);
        assert!(c.halted);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x101, "HALT itself advances PC and then holds");
        // A halted CPU keeps consuming T-states.
        assert_eq!(c.step(&mut m), 4, "and keeps ticking while halted");
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x101, "without advancing");

        c.irq = true;
        c.ack_irq(&mut m);
        assert!(!c.halted, "acceptance leaves the halt");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFF], 0x01, "and pushes 0x101 -- past the HALT");
        assert_eq!(m.ram[0x2FFE], 0x01);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0038);
    }

    /// Mode 2 masks the vector's low bit.
    ///
    /// The table holds 16-bit entries, so an odd bus value would read a misaligned
    /// pair — half of one vector and half of the next. Devices routinely put an odd
    /// value on the bus, so the mask is load-bearing rather than defensive.
    #[test]
    fn mode_two_masks_the_low_bit_of_the_vector() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 2;
        c.i = 0x40;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        m.irq_vector = 0x0F; // odd
        m.ram[0x400E] = 0x34; // the entry at 0x400E, not 0x400F
        m.ram[0x400F] = 0x12;
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), Some(19));
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x1234, "read from I<<8 | 0x0E");
    }

    /// Mode 0 executes the byte on the bus; mode 1 is `RST 38h` and ignores it.
    #[test]
    fn mode_zero_executes_the_bus_byte_and_mode_one_ignores_it() {
        // Mode 1: the bus byte is irrelevant.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 1;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        m.irq_vector = 0xC7; // RST 00h, which mode 1 must not honour
        c.ack_irq(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0038, "always 0x38");

        // Mode 0: the byte is an opcode, and CPS-1's board puts an RST there.
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.sp = 0x3000;
        c.im = 0;
        c.iff1 = true;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        m.irq_vector = 0xD7; // RST 10h
        c.ack_irq(&mut m);
        assert_eq!(c.pc, 0x0010, "the bus byte chose the vector");
    }

    /// A request with `IFF1` clear is held, not dropped.
    ///
    /// The line is level-sensitive: the device holds it until acknowledged. Clearing
    /// `irq` on a rejected check would lose the interrupt, which is a class of bug
    /// that presents as occasional missing sound rather than as a failure.
    #[test]
    fn a_rejected_request_stays_pending() {
        let mut c = Z80::new();
        c.pc = 0x200;
        c.im = 1;
        c.iff1 = false;
        c.irq = true;
        let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x200, &[0x00]);
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), None);
        assert!(c.irq, "still asserted -- the device holds the line");
        c.iff1 = true;
        assert_eq!(c.ack_irq(&mut m), Some(13), "and it lands when unmasked");
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Add the two request lines to `Z80`**

`irq` and `nmi` are **not** among the suite's 26 state fields — no vector case has a
pending interrupt — but they are how D2 asserts the sound board's interrupt line, so
they belong on the struct rather than in a wrapper. Add to `cpu.rs`, beside `halted`:

```rust
    /// The maskable interrupt request line.
    ///
    /// **Level-sensitive**, unlike [`Self::nmi`]: the device holds it asserted until
    /// the CPU acknowledges, so a refused request stays pending and only acceptance
    /// clears it. Not one of the suite's 26 fields — no vector case has an interrupt
    /// pending — so `diff_state` must not compare it, and
    /// `all_twenty_six_fields_are_compared`'s count of 26 stays 26.
    pub irq: bool,

    /// The non-maskable interrupt request line.
    ///
    /// **Edge-triggered**: acceptance consumes it. The asymmetry with `irq` is the
    /// chip's, and collapsing the two into one flag would either drop maskable
    /// interrupts or re-enter the NMI handler forever.
    pub nmi: bool,
```

`Z80::new` and `reset` leave both `false`. `reset` must **not** clear them: a request
asserted while the CPU is held in reset is still asserted when it comes out, and
clearing them there would silently drop the first interrupt after every reset.

- [ ] **Step 3: Add `irq_vector` to the test bus**

`crates/z80/src/testbus.rs` gains `pub irq_vector: u8` (default `0xFF`) and
`Mem::irq_vector` is what `Bus::irq_ack` returns. The `Bus` trait gains one
method with a default:

```rust
    /// The byte a device puts on the data bus during interrupt acknowledge.
    ///
    /// Defaults to `0xFF`, which in mode 0 is `RST 38h` — the same vector mode 1
    /// uses, so a bus that does not implement this behaves sanely rather than
    /// jumping somewhere arbitrary.
    fn irq_ack(&mut self) -> u8 {
        0xFF
    }
```

A default method is right here and a fifth required method is not: `TraceBus` and
every D2 consumer would otherwise have to implement a method the vectors never
exercise, and the default is a real, documented behaviour rather than a stub.

- [ ] **Step 4: Write `interrupt.rs`**

```rust
//! Interrupt acceptance: NMI, the three maskable modes, and the `EI` delay.
//!
//! **Nothing in the vector suite reaches this file.** Every one of the 1,604,000
//! cases is a single instruction with no interrupt, so the suite pins the `ei`,
//! `iff1`, `iff2` and `im` *fields* — a core that stores them wrongly is caught —
//! but never exercises acceptance. The tests in this module and the `z80int`
//! mutation set are the whole of this file's verification, which is why each rule
//! below names the failure it prevents.

use crate::{Bus, Z80};

impl Z80 {
    /// Services whichever request outranks the other, or neither.
    ///
    /// Returns the T-states consumed. NMI wins against a simultaneous maskable
    /// request, and the maskable one stays pending.
    pub fn service<B: Bus>(&mut self, bus: &mut B) -> u32 {
        if let Some(t) = self.ack_nmi(bus) {
            return t;
        }
        self.ack_irq(bus).unwrap_or(0)
    }

    /// Accepts a non-maskable interrupt if one is pending.
    ///
    /// NMI ignores `IFF1` — that is what non-maskable means — and **saves `IFF1`
    /// into `IFF2`** before clearing it. `RETN` copies it back. Without that save an
    /// NMI would permanently disable maskable interrupts, so the machine would work
    /// until the first NMI and then go quiet.
    pub fn ack_nmi<B: Bus>(&mut self, bus: &mut B) -> Option<u32> {
        if !self.nmi {
            return None;
        }
        self.nmi = false; // edge-triggered, unlike `irq`
        self.leave_halt();
        self.iff2 = self.iff1;
        self.iff1 = false;
        self.ei = 0;
        crate::ops::load::push(self, bus, self.pc);
        self.pc = 0x0066;
        Some(11)
    }

    /// Accepts a maskable interrupt if one is pending and unmasked.
    ///
    /// Returns `None` without touching `self.irq` when the request is refused: the
    /// line is level-sensitive and the device holds it until acknowledged. Clearing
    /// it here would drop interrupts intermittently — a bug that presents as
    /// occasionally missing sound rather than as a failure.
    pub fn ack_irq<B: Bus>(&mut self, bus: &mut B) -> Option<u32> {
        // `ei` is the one-instruction arming delay: `EI` sets it, and the *next*
        // instruction clears it. While it is set the interrupt is not yet enabled,
        // so `EI; RETI` cannot re-enter its own ISR.
        if !self.irq || !self.iff1 || self.ei != 0 {
            return None;
        }
        self.irq = false;
        self.leave_halt();
        self.iff1 = false;
        self.iff2 = false;
        let vector = bus.irq_ack();
        match self.im {
            0 => {
                // The bus byte is an opcode. In practice it is always an `RST`, and
                // that is the only form this implements -- executing an arbitrary
                // opcode from the acknowledge cycle is a path no board uses and the
                // suite cannot check.
                let target = u16::from(vector & 0x38);
                crate::ops::load::push(self, bus, self.pc);
                self.pc = target;
                Some(13)
            }
            1 => {
                crate::ops::load::push(self, bus, self.pc);
                self.pc = 0x0038;
                Some(13)
            }
            _ => {
                // The table holds 16-bit entries, so the low bit is masked: an odd
                // bus value would read half of one vector and half of the next.
                let addr = u16::from(self.i) << 8 | u16::from(vector & 0xFE);
                crate::ops::load::push(self, bus, self.pc);
                let lo = bus.read(addr);
                let hi = bus.read(addr.wrapping_add(1));
                self.pc = u16::from(hi) << 8 | u16::from(lo);
                Some(19)
            }
        }
    }

    /// Leaves the halted state.
    ///
    /// `PC` is **already** past the `HALT`: the instruction advanced it and then
    /// stopped advancing. So there is nothing to adjust here, and adjusting would be
    /// the bug — the ISR would return into the `HALT` and the machine would freeze
    /// the first time an interrupt arrived in an idle loop, which is where a sound
    /// CPU spends most of its time.
    fn leave_halt(&mut self) {
        self.halted = false;
    }
}
```

**Note on mode 0:** masking the vector to `& 0x38` implements the `RST` family and
nothing else. Say so in the doc comment as written above rather than pretending to
general opcode execution — a `match` on all 256 possible bus bytes would be dead
code no board reaches and no test can distinguish.

- [ ] **Step 5: Consume the `EI` arming in `step`**

In `cpu.rs`, at the top of `step`, after the halted check:

```rust
        // The EI arming lasts exactly one instruction. Snapshot it before dispatch
        // and clear it after, so an `EI` executed *by* this instruction survives.
        let was_armed = self.ei != 0;
        let t = /* dispatch */;
        if was_armed {
            self.ei = 0;
            self.iff1 = true;
            self.iff2 = true;
        }
        t
```

**Note on the ordering:** snapshotting before dispatch is what makes `EI; EI; NOP`
behave — the second `EI` re-arms and its own arming is not consumed by itself.
Clearing `self.ei` unconditionally after dispatch would swallow the arming that
`EI` just set, and the interrupt would never become enabled at all.

- [ ] **Step 7: The gate, and commit**

```bash
cargo fmt --all && cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "feat(z80): interrupts, NMI, and the EI delay

Nothing in the vector suite reaches this file: all 1,604,000 cases are
single instructions with no interrupt. The suite pins the ei/iff1/iff2/im
fields, so wrong storage is caught, but acceptance is verified only by
the tests here and by the z80int mutation set.

EI arms for after the next instruction; DI takes effect immediately. That
asymmetry is what lets an ISR end EI; RETI without re-entering itself.
NMI ignores IFF1 and saves it into IFF2 for RETN -- without the save, the
first NMI would silence maskable interrupts forever.

Acceptance leaves HALT with PC already past it. Pushing the HALT's own
address would make the ISR return into it and freeze the machine the
first time an interrupt arrived in an idle loop, which is where a sound
CPU spends most of its time. Mode 2 masks the vector's low bit: the table
is 16-bit entries and an odd bus value would read a misaligned pair.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 14: The disassembler

**Files:**
- Modify: `crates/z80/src/disasm.rs`

**Interfaces:**
- Produces: `disasm::disasm(read: impl FnMut(u16) -> u8, pc: u16) -> (String, u16)`.

### What this is for, and the one property that matters

D2 puts a Z80 pane in the debugger overlay, so it needs text for an address and
the length to advance by. The property that makes it useful rather than
decorative: **the returned length must equal the length `step` consumed.** A
disassembler that is one byte short desynchronises the whole pane, and every
line after the mistake is garbage — the failure mode is not "one wrong line".

So this task's central test is not a table of expected strings. It is a
**cross-check against the core**: run `step` on a synthetic instruction, run
`disasm` on the same bytes, and assert the two agree on the length. That test
cannot be satisfied by a plausible-looking table.

The crate is `no_std`-compatible, so the signature returns a fixed buffer rather
than a `String`:

```rust
pub fn disasm(read: impl FnMut(u16) -> u8, pc: u16) -> (Text, u16)
```

where `Text` is a small inline buffer with a `Display` impl. `alloc` is not
available and adding it for a debugger convenience would put an allocator
requirement on the WASM build.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

```rust
    /// The length `disasm` reports equals the length `step` consumes.
    ///
    /// This is the property that makes the pane usable: one byte short and every line
    /// after the mistake is garbage, not just the one. It is checked against the core
    /// rather than against a table, because a table of expected lengths is a second
    /// implementation of the same guess and would agree with a wrong one.
    #[test]
    fn the_reported_length_always_matches_what_step_consumes() {
        // A spread across all seven pages, including the pathological ones.
        let programs: &[&[u8]] = &[
            &[0x00],                          // NOP
            &[0x3E, 0x42],                    // LD A,n
            &[0x21, 0x34, 0x12],              // LD HL,nn
            &[0xC3, 0x00, 0x20],              // JP nn
            &[0x18, 0xFE],                    // JR d
            &[0xCB, 0x06],                    // RLC (HL)
            &[0xED, 0xB0],                    // LDIR
            &[0xED, 0x43, 0x00, 0x20],        // LD (nn),BC
            &[0xDD, 0x00],                    // prefixed NOP
            &[0xDD, 0x21, 0x34, 0x12],        // LD IX,nn
            &[0xDD, 0x7E, 0x05],              // LD A,(IX+5)
            &[0xDD, 0x36, 0x05, 0x99],        // LD (IX+5),n
            &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x05, 0x06],        // RLC (IX+5)
            &[0xFD, 0xCB, 0xFB, 0x46],        // BIT 0,(IY-5)
            &[0x76],                          // HALT
        ];
        for bytes in programs {
            let mut c = Z80::new();
            c.pc = 0x100;
            // SP well away from the program, so a CALL does not overwrite it.
            c.sp = 0x3000;
            let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, bytes);
            let (_, len) = disasm(|a| m.ram[a as usize], 0x100);
            c.step(&mut m);
            let consumed = c.pc.wrapping_sub(0x100);
            assert_eq!(
                u16::from(len as u8),
                consumed,
                "disasm and step disagree on {bytes:02X?}"
            );
        }
    }
```

**Note on that test's `consumed`:** it only works for instructions that do not
change `PC` — a `JP` sets `PC` to its target, so `c.pc - 0x100` is not the length.
Fix by comparing against a **non-branching** program set and testing branch
lengths separately, or better: have the test read `c.pc` only for the
non-branching entries and check the branching ones against `disasm`'s length plus
an explicit expected value. Write it the first way:

```rust
    #[test]
    fn the_reported_length_always_matches_what_step_consumes() {
        // Non-branching only: a JP sets PC to its target, so PC-delta is not a
        // length for those. The branching forms are covered by
        // `a_branch_reports_its_encoded_length`.
        let programs: &[&[u8]] = &[
            &[0x00],
            &[0x3E, 0x42],
            &[0x21, 0x34, 0x12],
            &[0xCB, 0x06],
            &[0xED, 0xB0],                    // LDIR with BC=0 does not repeat
            &[0xED, 0x43, 0x00, 0x20],
            &[0xDD, 0x00],
            &[0xDD, 0x21, 0x34, 0x12],
            &[0xDD, 0x7E, 0x05],
            &[0xDD, 0x36, 0x05, 0x99],
            &[0xDD, 0xCB, 0x05, 0x06],
            &[0xFD, 0xCB, 0xFB, 0x46],
        ];
        for bytes in programs {
            let mut c = Z80::new();
            c.pc = 0x100;
            c.sp = 0x3000;
            c.set_bc(0); // so LDIR runs its single non-repeating iteration
            let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, bytes);
            let (_, len) = disasm(|a| m.ram[a as usize], 0x100);
            c.step(&mut m);
            assert_eq!(len, c.pc.wrapping_sub(0x100), "on {bytes:02X?}");
        }
    }

    /// A branch's reported length is its encoding's length, not its target.
    #[test]
    fn a_branch_reports_its_encoded_length() {
        for (bytes, want) in [
            (&[0xC3u8, 0x00, 0x20][..], 3u16),
            (&[0x18, 0xFE][..], 2),
            (&[0xC9][..], 1),
            (&[0xCD, 0x00, 0x20][..], 3),
            (&[0xC7][..], 1),
            (&[0xE9][..], 1),
            (&[0xDD, 0xE9][..], 2),
        ] {
            let mut m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, bytes);
            let (text, len) = disasm(|a| m.ram[a as usize], 0x100);
            assert_eq!(len, want, "{bytes:02X?} disassembled as {text}");
        }
    }

    /// The text is readable, and the displacement is shown signed.
    ///
    /// `(IX-5)`, not `(IX+251)` and not `(IX+fb)`. A debugger that shows the raw byte
    /// makes the reader do the two's-complement conversion, which is exactly the
    /// conversion they opened the debugger to avoid.
    #[test]
    fn the_text_reads_like_assembly_with_a_signed_displacement() {
        for (bytes, want) in [
            (&[0x00u8][..], "nop"),
            (&[0x3E, 0x42][..], "ld a,$42"),
            (&[0x21, 0x34, 0x12][..], "ld hl,$1234"),
            (&[0xCB, 0x06][..], "rlc (hl)"),
            (&[0xED, 0xB0][..], "ldir"),
            (&[0xDD, 0x7E, 0x05][..], "ld a,(ix+5)"),
            (&[0xDD, 0x7E, 0xFB][..], "ld a,(ix-5)"),
            (&[0xFD, 0xCB, 0xFB, 0x46][..], "bit 0,(iy-5)"),
            (&[0x18, 0xFE][..], "jr $0100"),
        ] {
            let m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, bytes);
            let (text, _) = disasm(|a| m.ram[a as usize], 0x100);
            assert_eq!(text.as_str(), want, "on {bytes:02X?}");
        }
    }

    /// `jr` shows its resolved target, not its displacement.
    ///
    /// `18 FE` is a jump to itself: the displacement is relative to the *end* of the
    /// instruction, so −2 from 0x102 is 0x100. Showing `jr -2` would make the reader
    /// do that arithmetic, and getting the base wrong by one is the classic error.
    #[test]
    fn jr_resolves_its_target_from_the_end_of_the_instruction() {
        let m = crate::testbus::Mem::at(0x100, &[0x18, 0x05]);
        let (text, len) = disasm(|a| m.ram[a as usize], 0x100);
        assert_eq!(len, 2);
        assert_eq!(text.as_str(), "jr $0107", "0x102 + 5, not 0x100 + 5");
    }

    /// `Text` truncates rather than panicking.
    ///
    /// A debugger pane must never take the emulator down. The longest real
    /// instruction text is well inside the buffer, so truncation is unreachable in
    /// practice — which is exactly why it must be tested directly rather than
    /// trusted.
    #[test]
    fn the_text_buffer_truncates_instead_of_panicking() {
        let mut t = Text::new();
        for _ in 0..200 {
            t.push_str("xxxxxxxxxx");
        }
        assert_eq!(t.as_str().len(), Text::CAP);
    }
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `Text`**

```rust
/// A fixed-capacity string, so the disassembler needs no allocator.
///
/// `alloc` is not available in this crate and adding it for a debugger convenience
/// would put an allocator requirement on the WASM build. 32 bytes is over twice the
/// longest real instruction text (`bit 0,(iy-128)` is 15).
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
pub struct Text {
    buf: [u8; Self::CAP],
    len: usize,
}

impl Text {
    pub const CAP: usize = 32;

    #[must_use]
    pub fn new() -> Self {
        Self { buf: [0; Self::CAP], len: 0 }
    }

    /// Appends, **truncating** if full.
    ///
    /// A debugger pane must never take the emulator down, and the alternative to
    /// truncation is a panic on a path no test would otherwise reach.
    pub fn push_str(&mut self, s: &str) {
        for &b in s.as_bytes() {
            if self.len == Self::CAP {
                return;
            }
            self.buf[self.len] = b;
            self.len += 1;
        }
    }

    /// Appends `v` as lower-case hex, `width` digits, zero-padded.
    pub fn push_hex(&mut self, v: u32, width: usize) {
        const D: &[u8; 16] = b"0123456789abcdef";
        for i in (0..width).rev() {
            let nib = (v >> (i * 4)) & 0xF;
            self.push_str(core::str::from_utf8(&D[nib as usize..=nib as usize]).unwrap_or("?"));
        }
    }

    /// Appends a signed decimal, for displacements.
    pub fn push_i8(&mut self, v: i8) {
        if v < 0 {
            self.push_str("-");
        }
        let m = v.unsigned_abs();
        if m >= 100 {
            self.push_hex(u32::from(m / 100), 1);
        }
        // ... (decimal, not hex -- see the note below)
    }

    #[must_use]
    pub fn as_str(&self) -> &str {
        core::str::from_utf8(&self.buf[..self.len]).unwrap_or("<bad utf8>")
    }
}

impl Default for Text {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Self::new()
    }
}

impl core::fmt::Display for Text {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut core::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> core::fmt::Result {
        f.write_str(self.as_str())
    }
}

impl core::fmt::Write for Text {
    fn write_str(&mut self, s: &str) -> core::fmt::Result {
        self.push_str(s);
        Ok(())
    }
}
```

**Note on `push_hex` and `push_i8` as drafted above:** both are wrong as written —
`push_hex` builds a one-character `&str` through a `from_utf8` that can fail, and
`push_i8` is half-finished and mixes hex into a decimal. Implement the `Write` impl
first and then write both in terms of it:

```rust
    pub fn push_hex(&mut self, v: u32, width: usize) {
        use core::fmt::Write as _;
        let _ = write!(self, "{v:0width$x}");
    }

    pub fn push_i8(&mut self, v: i8) {
        use core::fmt::Write as _;
        let _ = write!(self, "{v}");
    }
```

`core::fmt::Write` is available in `no_std`, the `Write` impl above cannot fail, and
this deletes both hand-rolled formatters. The discarded `Result` is deliberate:
truncation is the documented behaviour and there is nothing to report.

- [ ] **Step 4: Write `disasm`**

```rust
/// Disassembles one instruction at `pc`.
///
/// Returns the text and the instruction's **length in bytes**. That length must
/// equal what [`Z80::step`] consumes: a disassembler one byte short desynchronises
/// a debugger pane so that every subsequent line is garbage, not merely the one.
/// `the_reported_length_always_matches_what_step_consumes` checks this against the
/// core rather than against a table of expected lengths, which would be a second
/// guess capable of agreeing with a wrong first one.
///
/// `read` is a closure rather than a `&mut impl Bus` so a caller can disassemble
/// without a mutable borrow of the machine — the debugger renders while paused and
/// must not perturb bus state, and some bus reads have side effects.
pub fn disasm(mut read: impl FnMut(u16) -> u8, pc: u16) -> (Text, u16) {
    let mut t = Text::new();
    let mut n = 0u16;
    let mut next = |t: &mut Text| {
        let _ = t;
        let b = read(pc.wrapping_add(n));
        n = n.wrapping_add(1);
        b
    };
    // ... dispatch, appending to `t` and reading operands through `next`
    (t, n)
}
```

**Note on the closure:** the `next` closure above captures `n` mutably while `t` is
also borrowed, which will not compile as written. Write it as a small struct
instead:

```rust
struct Cur<F> {
    read: F,
    pc: u16,
    n: u16,
}

impl<F: FnMut(u16) -> u8> Cur<F> {
    fn byte(&mut self) -> u8 {
        let b = (self.read)(self.pc.wrapping_add(self.n));
        self.n = self.n.wrapping_add(1);
        b
    }

    fn word(&mut self) -> u16 {
        let lo = self.byte();
        let hi = self.byte();
        u16::from(hi) << 8 | u16::from(lo)
    }
}
```

Then `disasm` owns a `Cur` and a `Text` as separate locals, and the borrow problem
does not arise. Do not reach for `RefCell` — restructuring is the fix.

The dispatch itself mirrors `decode.rs`'s structure: the same page split, the same
register tables (`b c d e h l (hl) a`), the same `alu_op` order
(`add adc sub sbc and xor or cp`), and the same condition names
(`nz z nc c po pe p m`). Keeping the two files structurally parallel is what makes
a divergence visible when someone edits one of them.

- [ ] **Step 5: Run the tests**

```bash
cargo test -p z80 --lib disasm
cargo test -p z80 --lib
```

- [ ] **Step 6: The gate, and commit**

```bash
cargo fmt --all && cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/z80
git commit -m "feat(z80): one-instruction disassembly for D2's debugger pane

The property that matters is not the text -- it is that the reported
length equals what step consumes. One byte short and every following
line of the pane is garbage, not just the one. So the central test
cross-checks disasm against the core on a spread across all seven pages,
rather than against a table of expected lengths that would be a second
guess able to agree with a wrong first one.

Fixed-capacity Text rather than String: alloc is unavailable here and
adding it for a debugger convenience would put an allocator requirement
on the WASM build. It truncates rather than panicking, because a
debugger pane must never take the emulator down.

Displacements print signed and jr prints its resolved target, computed
from the end of the instruction -- the two conversions a reader opens a
disassembler to avoid doing by hand.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 15: The suite harness and the report binary

**Files:**
- Create: `crates/testrunner/tests/z80suite.rs`
- Create: `crates/testrunner/src/bin/reportz80.rs`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `z80files::{Page, all_names, page_counts, stem}`, `z80runner::run_file`,
  `z80fmt::parse_file`.
- Produces: nothing later tasks build on. This is the gate.

### Eleven tests, not 1,604

The spec says "one test per file, generated by a macro over the file list." The
68000 suite does exactly that with 127 `groups!` entries, hand-written. **1,604
hand-written entries is not viable**, and generating them from `read_dir` at
compile time would make the test list depend on what happens to be on disk — a
suite that silently shrinks when the fetch is incomplete.

So the granularity moves down one level: **eight page tests plus three coverage
tests.** A failure still names the file, because `run_file` panics with the
filename in the message — the spec's actual requirement ("a failure names
`dd_cb_06` rather than reporting 'Z80 broken'") is met by the panic message, not
by the test name.

| test | files |
|------|-------|
| `base_00_7f` | 128 |
| `base_80_ff` | 124 |
| `cb_page` | 256 |
| `ed_page` | 80 |
| `dd_page` | 252 |
| `fd_page` | 252 |
| `ddcb_page` | 256 |
| `fdcb_page` | 256 |
| **total** | **1,604** |

The base page splits at 0x80 only so the two halves can run concurrently under
`cargo test`'s default thread pool; the boundary has no meaning beyond that.

### The three coverage tests are the substance

A page test over an **empty directory** would iterate nothing and report success.
That is this project's recurring vacuous-pass shape, and it is why three tests
exist whose only job is to make emptiness and incompleteness fail:

- `every_vector_file_is_covered` — every name in `all_names()` is claimed by
  exactly one page, and every `.z80bin` on disk corresponds to a name.
- `every_page_has_its_expected_file_count` — the per-page counts are **literals**
  in the test, never `read_dir().count()`. A count read from disk would agree with
  whatever is there, including nothing.
- `no_page_is_empty` — the cheapest possible statement of the failure the other
  two are shaped around, kept separate so that a wholly missing `testdata/z80`
  fails with one clear message rather than eight.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write `tests/z80suite.rs`**

```rust
//! The Z80 vector suite: 1,604 files, 1,604,000 cases.
//!
//! Eight page tests, not 1,604 file tests. The 68000 suite hand-writes 127 macro
//! entries; 1,604 is not viable, and generating the list from `read_dir` at compile
//! time would make the test set depend on what happens to be on disk — a suite that
//! silently shrinks when the fetch is incomplete.
//!
//! A failure still names its file: [`run_file`] panics with the filename in the
//! message. And the three coverage tests below are what stop a page test over an
//! empty directory from reporting success.

use testrunner::z80files::{self, Page};
use testrunner::z80runner;

/// Runs every file belonging to `page`, and returns how many cases passed.
fn run_page(page: Page) -> usize {
    let names: Vec<&'static str> = z80files::all_names()
        .iter()
        .copied()
        .filter(|n| z80files::page_of(n) == page)
        .collect();
    assert!(
        !names.is_empty(),
        "no files claimed by {page:?} -- the inventory is wrong, not the data"
    );
    let mut cases = 0;
    for name in names {
        cases += z80runner::run_file(name);
    }
    cases
}

#[test]
fn base_00_7f() {
    // The base page splits at 0x80 only so the halves run concurrently under
    // cargo test's thread pool. The boundary means nothing else.
    let n: usize = z80files::all_names()
        .iter()
        .filter(|s| z80files::page_of(s) == Page::Base && s.as_bytes()[0] < b'8')
        .map(|s| z80runner::run_file(s))
        .sum();
    assert_eq!(n, 128 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn base_80_ff() {
    let n: usize = z80files::all_names()
        .iter()
        .filter(|s| z80files::page_of(s) == Page::Base && s.as_bytes()[0] >= b'8')
        .map(|s| z80runner::run_file(s))
        .sum();
    assert_eq!(n, 124 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn cb_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::Cb), 256 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn ed_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::Ed), 80 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn dd_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::Dd), 252 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn fd_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::Fd), 252 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn ddcb_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::DdCb), 256 * 1000);
}

#[test]
fn fdcb_page() {
    assert_eq!(run_page(Page::FdCb), 256 * 1000);
}

/// Every file on disk is a file the inventory knows, and vice versa.
///
/// Without this, a file the fetcher wrote under a name `all_names` does not produce
/// would sit in `testdata/z80` untested forever, and the suite would report full
/// coverage.
#[test]
fn every_vector_file_is_covered() {
    let dir = z80files::dir();
    let mut on_disk: Vec<String> = std::fs::read_dir(&dir)
        .unwrap_or_else(|e| panic!("missing {}: {e}\n{}", dir.display(), z80files::FETCH_HINT))
        .filter_map(|e| e.ok())
        .filter_map(|e| e.file_name().into_string().ok())
        .filter(|n| n.ends_with(".z80bin"))
        .map(|n| n.trim_end_matches(".z80bin").to_string())
        .collect();
    on_disk.sort();

    let mut expected: Vec<String> =
        z80files::all_names().iter().map(|n| z80files::stem(n)).collect();
    expected.sort();

    assert_eq!(
        on_disk, expected,
        "the files on disk and the inventory disagree; run the fetcher"
    );
}

/// Each page's file count is a **literal** here, never `read_dir().count()`.
///
/// A count read from disk agrees with whatever is on disk, including nothing — which
/// is precisely the failure the eight page tests above cannot see, because a loop
/// over an empty list passes.
#[test]
fn every_page_has_its_expected_file_count() {
    for (page, want) in [
        (Page::Base, 252usize),
        (Page::Cb, 256),
        (Page::Ed, 80),
        (Page::Dd, 252),
        (Page::Fd, 252),
        (Page::DdCb, 256),
        (Page::FdCb, 256),
    ] {
        let n = z80files::all_names().iter().filter(|s| z80files::page_of(s) == page).count();
        assert_eq!(n, want, "{page:?}");
    }
    assert_eq!(z80files::all_names().len(), 1604, "and 1,604 in total");
}

/// The cheapest statement of the failure the other two are shaped around.
///
/// Kept separate so that a wholly absent `testdata/z80` fails once, clearly, rather
/// than eight times with eight different case-count mismatches.
#[test]
fn no_page_is_empty() {
    for page in Page::ALL {
        let files: Vec<_> =
            z80files::all_names().iter().filter(|s| z80files::page_of(s) == *page).collect();
        assert!(!files.is_empty(), "{page:?} claims no files");
        let first = z80files::dir().join(format!("{}.z80bin", z80files::stem(files[0])));
        assert!(
            first.exists(),
            "{} is missing\n{}",
            first.display(),
            z80files::FETCH_HINT
        );
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Add `dir` and `FETCH_HINT` to `z80files.rs`**

```rust
/// The single fetch command, in one place.
///
/// Every loud failure quotes this. Duplicating the string across the harness is how
/// one copy goes stale and sends a reader to a command that no longer exists.
pub const FETCH_HINT: &str =
    "run `cargo run -p testrunner --release --bin fetchz80` to download the vectors";

/// Where the converted vectors live.
#[must_use]
pub fn dir() -> std::path::PathBuf {
    crate::repo_root().join("testdata/z80")
}
```


This is the step that proves the loud-failure path works. Run it before fetching,

```bash
time cargo test -p testrunner --release --test z80suite
```

Expected: 11 passed. On the measured 68000 throughput (~660k cases/sec,
single-threaded) this is roughly 3 s of comparison plus the file reads.

**If it exceeds ~60 s, that is a finding to report, not to work around.** Do not
add `rayon`, do not add a subset mode. At that point something is quadratic and the
right move is to find it.

- [ ] **Step 6: Write `bin/reportz80.rs`**

```rust
//! Reports the Z80 suite the way `report --test suite` reports the 68000 one:
//! `groups: N/N green   cases: M/M`.
//!
//! Separate from the test harness because a `#[test]` cannot print a summary line —
//! it can only pass or fail — and the one number a human wants after a change is the
//! count, not a list of eleven `ok`s.

use testrunner::{z80files, z80runner};

fn main() {
    let names = z80files::all_names();
    let mut green = 0usize;
    let mut cases = 0usize;
    let mut failed: Vec<&str> = Vec::new();

    for name in names {
        match std::panic::catch_unwind(|| z80runner::run_file(name)) {
            Ok(n) => {
                green += 1;
                cases += n;
            }
            Err(_) => failed.push(name),
        }
    }

    println!("groups: {green}/{}   cases: {cases}/{}", names.len(), names.len() * 1000);
    if !failed.is_empty() {
        // Cap the list: 1,604 failures would bury the summary line that the reader
        // came for, and the first few name the same defect as the rest.
        println!("failed ({}):", failed.len());
        for n in failed.iter().take(20) {
            println!("  {n}");
        }
        if failed.len() > 20 {
            println!("  ... and {} more", failed.len() - 20);
        }
        std::process::exit(1);
    }
}
```

**Note on `catch_unwind`:** `run_file` panics on a mismatch, and this binary needs
to survive one to count the rest. Set a panic hook that suppresses the default
message first, or the output is 1,604 backtraces:

```rust
    let quiet = std::panic::take_hook();
    std::panic::set_hook(Box::new(|_| {}));
    // ... the loop ...
    std::panic::set_hook(quiet);
```

`run_file` takes `&'static str` and captures nothing that is not `UnwindSafe`, so
no `AssertUnwindSafe` is needed. If the compiler disagrees, the fix is to make
`run_file` return `Result` rather than to wrap the closure — a suite runner that
reports failures as values is better than one that reports them as unwinds, and the
`#[test]`s can `unwrap` it.

- [ ] **Step 7: Run the report**

```bash
cargo run -p testrunner --release --bin reportz80
```

Expected: `groups: 1604/1604   cases: 1604000/1604000`.

**If a group fails, the vectors are right and the core is wrong.** Read the panic
message, fix the core, re-run. Do not add the group to a skip list and do not lower
the target — that decision is not available at this level.

- [ ] **Step 8: Confirm the 68000 suite is untouched**

```bash
cargo run -p testrunner --release --bin report -- --test suite
```

Expected: `127/127`, `317500/317500`. D1 modifies no shared code, so any movement
is a regression to investigate.

```bash
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add crates/testrunner
git commit -m "test(z80): the vector suite at 1604/1604, 1604000/1604000

Eight page tests plus three coverage tests, not 1,604 file tests. The
68000 suite hand-writes 127 macro entries; 1,604 is not viable, and
generating the list from read_dir at compile time would make the test set
depend on what is on disk -- a suite that silently shrinks when the fetch
is incomplete. A failure still names its file, via run_file's panic.

The three coverage tests are the substance. A page test over an empty
directory iterates nothing and reports success, which is this project's
recurring vacuous-pass shape. So the per-page counts are literals, never
read_dir().count(), which would agree with whatever is there including
```

## Task 16: The mutation sets `z80flags` and `z80int`

**Files:**
- Modify: `scripts/mutate.py` (add two sets)

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: the whole crate as tracked source.
- Produces: nothing. This measures the tests written in Tasks 5–15.

### What a mutation pass is for here, and the rule it must not break

The vector suite proves the core matches real hardware. It does **not** prove the
hand-written tests are worth anything — 1,604,000 vectors would pass with every
`#[test]` in the crate deleted. The mutation pass measures the tests, and it is
the only thing that does.

Two sets, because the two areas have opposite problems:

- **`z80flags`** — the flag rules *are* covered by vectors, so a mutant here dies
  either way. The question this set answers is whether the **hand-written** tests
  kill it, because if only the suite does, the crate has 40 flag tests that assert
  nothing and nobody will find out until someone breaks the suite for an unrelated
  reason and has no smaller signal to work with.
- **`z80int`** — nothing in the suite reaches `interrupt.rs`. Here a surviving
  mutant means the behaviour is genuinely unverified.

**The binding rule from this project's memory, restated because it is the one a
mutation pass gets wrong:** write the killer before the mutant. For each mutation
below, the plan names the test that kills it. If that test does not exist yet,
**write it first** — choosing mutants is how you find the assertions that cannot
fail, and a mutant chosen after the fact just confirms what you already tested.

Three further rules, each from a memory of getting it wrong:

- **A mutation pass owns the tree.** No `cargo` runs alongside it: a concurrent
  build fakes kills. And do not leave a mutant in tracked source — commit before
  mutating, so `git checkout` reverts the mutant without destroying uncommitted
  work.
- **Run both sets, not one.** A control silently becomes a real mutant when a
  later task claims the bit it moves.
- **Score only the disagreeing cases.** A mutation scored against the full suite
  reads as exact while measuring nothing: every mutant dies against 1,604,000
  vectors. The number that matters is *which hand-written test* died, and the
  run must record that per mutant.

### `z80flags` — 12 mutants

| # | file | mutation | killed by |
|---|------|----------|-----------|
| 1 | `flags.rs` | `parity` returns `!p` | `every_cb_rotate_writes_the_full_flag_set` |
| 2 | `flags.rs` | `sz53` masks `0x28` instead of `0xA8` (drops S) | `sz53_carries_sign_and_the_two_undocumented_bits` |
| 3 | `ops/alu.rs` | `cp` takes F3/F5 from the result, not the operand | `cp_takes_the_undocumented_flags_from_the_operand` |
| 4 | `ops/alu.rs` | `inc8` clears carry | `inc_and_dec_preserve_the_carry_flag` |
| 5 | `ops/alu.rs` | `add16` takes H from bit 12 rather than bit 11 | `add16_takes_half_carry_from_bit_eleven` |
| 6 | `ops/alu.rs` | `add16` also writes S/Z | `add16_preserves_sign_zero_and_overflow` |
| 7 | `ops/bits.rs` | `bit_test` computes real parity instead of copying Z | `bit_puts_a_copy_of_zero_in_parity` |
| 8 | `ops/bits.rs` | `bit_test` sets S for every bit, not only bit 7 | `only_bit_seven_sets_the_sign_flag` |
| 9 | `ops/bits.rs` | `rlca` also writes S/Z/PV | `rlca_preserves_what_cb_zero_seven_writes` |
| 10 | `decode.rs` | `scf_ccf` always takes F3/F5 from `A` (ignores `Q`) | `scf_after_a_non_flag_writing_instruction_ors_with_the_old_flags` |
| 11 | `decode.rs` | `daa` ignores N | `daa_corrects_a_bcd_subtraction_because_n_is_set` |
| 12 | `ops/load.rs` | `ldi_ldd` puts real parity in P/V, not `BC != 0` | `ldi_moves_a_byte_and_puts_the_remaining_count_in_parity` |

### `z80int` — 8 mutants

| # | file | mutation | killed by |
|---|------|----------|-----------|
| 1 | `interrupt.rs` | `ack_irq` ignores `self.ei` | `ei_does_not_let_an_interrupt_in_until_after_the_next_instruction` |
| 2 | `interrupt.rs` | `ack_nmi` does not copy `iff1` into `iff2` | `nmi_ignores_iff1_but_saves_it_for_retn` |
| 3 | `interrupt.rs` | `ack_irq` clears `self.irq` before the mask check | `a_rejected_request_stays_pending` |
| 4 | `interrupt.rs` | mode 2 does not mask the vector's low bit | `mode_two_masks_the_low_bit_of_the_vector` |
| 5 | `interrupt.rs` | `leave_halt` also does `pc -= 1` | `an_interrupt_leaves_halt_with_pc_past_the_halt_instruction` |
| 6 | `interrupt.rs` | `service` checks `ack_irq` before `ack_nmi` | `an_nmi_wins_against_a_simultaneous_maskable_interrupt` |
| 7 | `cpu.rs` | `step` clears `ei` unconditionally after dispatch | `ei_does_not_let_an_interrupt_in_until_after_the_next_instruction` |
| 8 | `decode.rs` | `RETN` does not restore `iff1` | `nmi_ignores_iff1_but_saves_it_for_retn` |

**Two declared controls, one per set**, so a pass of 20/20 is distinguishable from
a harness that reports success without running anything:

- `z80flags` control: `flags.rs`'s doc comment gains a word. Expected: **survives.**
- `z80int` control: `interrupt.rs`'s `service` gains `let _ = &bus;`. Expected:
  **survives**, since it changes no behaviour.

A control that dies means the harness is comparing something other than test
outcomes — investigate the harness, not the core.

- [ ] **Step 1: Verify every named killer exists**

```bash
for t in every_cb_rotate_writes_the_full_flag_set \
         sz53_carries_sign_and_the_two_undocumented_bits \
         cp_takes_the_undocumented_flags_from_the_operand \
         inc_and_dec_preserve_the_carry_flag \
         add16_takes_half_carry_from_bit_eleven \
         add16_preserves_sign_zero_and_overflow \
         bit_puts_a_copy_of_zero_in_parity \
         only_bit_seven_sets_the_sign_flag \
         rlca_preserves_what_cb_zero_seven_writes \
         scf_after_a_non_flag_writing_instruction_ors_with_the_old_flags \
         daa_corrects_a_bcd_subtraction_because_n_is_set \
         ldi_moves_a_byte_and_puts_the_remaining_count_in_parity \
         ei_does_not_let_an_interrupt_in_until_after_the_next_instruction \
         nmi_ignores_iff1_but_saves_it_for_retn \
         a_rejected_request_stays_pending \
         mode_two_masks_the_low_bit_of_the_vector \
         an_interrupt_leaves_halt_with_pc_past_the_halt_instruction \
         an_nmi_wins_against_a_simultaneous_maskable_interrupt ; do
  grep -rq "fn $t" crates/z80/src || echo "MISSING: $t"
done
```

Expected: **no output.** Any name printed is a killer that was named in this plan
and never written — write it now, before the mutation run. A mutant with no
declared killer is a mutant whose survival cannot be interpreted.

Two names in the tables above are not in the loop because they are written as part
of this task's Step 2 (they belong to functions whose tests Task 7 wrote under
different names): if `sz53_carries_sign_and_the_two_undocumented_bits`,
`add16_takes_half_carry_from_bit_eleven`, `add16_preserves_sign_zero_and_overflow`,
`cp_takes_the_undocumented_flags_from_the_operand`,
`inc_and_dec_preserve_the_carry_flag`, `bit_puts_a_copy_of_zero_in_parity`,
`only_bit_seven_sets_the_sign_flag` or `rlca_preserves_what_cb_zero_seven_writes`
print, reconcile the name with what Task 7 or 9 actually wrote and update **this
plan's table**, not the test's name — renaming a test to match a plan is how a
table stops describing the tree.

- [ ] **Step 2: Commit first**

```bash
git status --porcelain
```

Expected: **empty.** A mutation pass on a dirty tree cannot be reverted with
`git checkout` without destroying the uncommitted work in the same file. If it is
not empty, commit before continuing.

- [ ] **Step 3: Add the two sets to `scripts/mutate.py`**

Follow the file's existing structure exactly — each set is a list of
`(path, old, new, killer)` tuples, and the runner applies one, runs **only the named
killer's test**, reverts, and records. Running the whole suite per mutant would take
20 × 3 s of vectors to learn nothing: the suite kills every one of these, which is
the fact this pass is designed to look past.

- [ ] **Step 4: Run both sets, with nothing else touching the tree**

```bash
python3 scripts/mutate.py --set z80flags
python3 scripts/mutate.py --set z80int
```

Expected: 12/12 and 8/8 killed, plus one surviving control each.

- [ ] **Step 5: Interpret any survivor before fixing it**

A survivor is one of three things and the distinction matters:

1. **A declared control** — expected; record it.
2. **A proven equivalent** — the mutation genuinely cannot change behaviour.
   Prove it in writing in the commit message, do not assert it.
3. **A real gap** — the named killer does not actually test what its name claims.
   Fix the *test*, not the mutant list. This is the outcome the pass exists to
   produce, and finding one is a success.

- [ ] **Step 6: Restore the tree and commit**

```bash
git checkout crates/z80/src scripts/mutate.py 2>/dev/null
git status --porcelain   # expect: only scripts/mutate.py's real additions
git diff --stat
```

Then re-add the intended changes and commit:

```bash
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
git add scripts/mutate.py crates/z80
git commit -m "test(z80): mutation sets z80flags and z80int at 20/20

The vector suite proves the core matches hardware. It does not prove the
hand-written tests are worth anything -- all 1,604,000 cases would pass
with every #[test] in the crate deleted. This pass measures those tests,
and it is the only thing that does.

Two sets with opposite problems. z80flags mutates rules the vectors do
cover, so each mutant dies either way; the question is whether the
hand-written test kills it, because a crate with 40 flag tests that
assert nothing looks identical until someone needs a signal smaller than
the whole suite. z80int mutates interrupt.rs, which no vector reaches, so
a survivor there is genuinely unverified behaviour.

Each mutant's killer was verified to exist before the run: choosing
mutants is how you find the assertions that cannot fail, and one chosen
after the fact only confirms what you already tested. Each set carries a
declared control, so 20/20 is distinguishable from a harness that
reports success without running.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Task 17: README and the roadmap row

**Files:**
- Modify: `README.md`

**Interfaces:** none. Documentation only.

### What changes, and one thing that must not

The roadmap's `D` row becomes three rows, `D1` complete and `D2`/`D3` pending.
The crate table gains `z80`. The verification section gains the Z80 suite's
numbers beside the 68000's.

**What must not change:** nothing in this task adds a URL to a ROM, and nothing
describes where to obtain one. The README's existing statement — that the user
supplies a MAME-format ROM set at a path they own, legally from Capcom Arcade
Stadium, Capcom Fighting Collection, or a board they dumped — stands as written.
The vector suite's URL is fine: it is MIT-licensed test data, not game code.

**And: there is still no sound.** D1 is a CPU with no chip attached. Saying
otherwise in a README is the kind of claim that costs a reader an afternoon.

- [ ] **Step 1: Split the roadmap's `D` row**

```markdown
| A | 68000 core | done | 127/127 vector groups, 317,500 cases |
| B | Bus and ROM loader | done | CRC-verified MAME set at a runtime path |
| C | CPS-1 video | done | scanline renderer, tiles and sprites |
| D1 | Z80 core | **done** | 1,604/1,604 vector groups, 1,604,000 cases |
| D2 | YM2151 and sound-board wiring | pending | the latch reaches a chip |
| D3 | OKI MSM6295 and host audio | pending | **this is the one that makes sound** |
| E1 | Window and save states | done | |
| E2 | Debugger overlay | done | `F1` |
| E3 | Graphics viewers | done | `F9` / `F10` |
| F | SF1 driver | pending | |
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Add `z80` to the crate table**

```markdown
| `z80` | Zilog Z80 CPU. No dependencies, `no_std`-compatible. Owns no memory — the
caller implements `Bus`, which has four methods because the Z80 has a separate
16-bit I/O address space. |
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Add the suite to the verification section**

```markdown
### Z80

`SingleStepTests/z80` (MIT), 1,604 files × 1,000 cases = **1,604,000**, compared
per T-state: the 26 state fields, declared RAM, the T-state total, the bus trace's
address and pins, data on exactly the samples flagged valid, and the port
transactions in order.

The vectors are **not committed** — `testdata/` is gitignored, and the converted
form is ~236 MB. Fetch with:

    cargo run -p testrunner --release --bin fetchz80

A missing file fails loudly, naming the file and that command. There is no
environment-variable escape hatch and no `#[ignore]`.

    cargo run -p testrunner --release --bin reportz80
    groups: 1604/1604   cases: 1604000/1604000
```

- [ ] **Step 4: State plainly that D1 is silent**

Under the roadmap, one line:

```markdown
> **Sound:** D1 is a CPU, not audio. The Z80 executes the sound program, and
> nothing is connected to its ports yet — no YM2151, no OKI, no host audio device.
> The emulator is silent until D3.
```

- [ ] **Step 5: Verify no ROM URL was introduced**

```bash
grep -niE 'rom.?(set|zip).*(http|download|torrent)|sfii?\.zip.*http' README.md
```

Expected: **no output.** The rule has no exemption for documentation.

- [ ] **Step 6: The gate, and commit**

```bash
cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace && cargo test --workspace --release
cargo doc --no-deps --workspace
git add README.md
git commit -m "docs: D1 complete -- Z80 core at 1604/1604, and still no sound

Splits the roadmap's D row into D1/D2/D3, adds the z80 crate and the
vector suite's numbers, and says plainly that D1 is a CPU with nothing
attached to its ports. The emulator is silent until D3, and a README that
implied otherwise would cost a reader an afternoon.

No ROM URL added: the rule has no documentation exemption. The vector
suite's URL is MIT-licensed test data, not game code.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>"
```

## Definition of done

Copied from the spec, with the numbers this plan pinned:

1. `cargo test --workspace` and `--release` green.
2. `groups: 1604/1604   cases: 1604000/1604000`, per-T-state comparison enabled.
3. `cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings` clean.
4. `cargo doc --no-deps --workspace` clean.
5. The 68000 suite still at **127/127, 317,500/317,500**. D1 touches no shared
   code, so movement is a regression to investigate, not a tolerance.
6. Both mutation sets at 100% as-expected: 12/12 and 8/8, each with its declared
   control surviving.
7. `crates/z80` has no dependencies. `machine` is unchanged.

No group may be excluded, and no target may be lowered, without evidence
documented in the spec. As of writing no upstream group is known bad.
